is functor defined by the composition
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\mapsto\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D}\mapsto H_{k}(\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D})
.

For an abelian group
A
, the
categorical cohomology of
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
with coefficients in
A
, denoted
H_{\bowtie}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D};A)
, is the categorical cohomology of
\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D}
with coefficients in
A
.

We work with simplicial groups rather than chain complexes (see
[
Wei94
, Ch.8]
) to simplify calculations. The Dold–Kan Theorem
[
Wei94
, Theorem 8.4.1]
gives an equivalence of categories between simplicial abelian groups and chain complexes of abelian groups.

For each
k\geq 1
and
0\leq i\leq k+1
we define the
face map

\partial_{k}^{i}\colon C_{k+1}(\mathcal{C})\to C_{k}(\mathcal{C})
by

(4.1)

\partial_{k}^{i}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{i},\ldots,c_{k}]=\begin{cases}[c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}]&if $i=0$\\
[c_{0},\ldots,c_{i-1}c_{i},\ldots,c_{k}]&if $1\leq i\leq k$\\
[c_{0},\ldots,c_{k-1}]&if $i=k+1$.\end{cases}

We also define
\partial_{0}^{0}[c]=[s(c)]
and
\partial_{0}^{1}[c]=[r(c)]
. In particular,
d_{k}=\sum_{i=0}^{k+1}(-1)^{k}\partial_{k}^{i}
.

To work with degeneracy maps, we use the following—slightly non-standard—notation.

Notation 4.3
.

If
(c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})\in\mathcal{C}^{k}
is a composable
k
-tuple, and
0\leq i\leq k
, then we define

\displaystyle(c_{1},\ldots,c_{i-1},\_,c_{i+1},\ldots,c_{k})

\displaystyle\coloneqq(c_{1},\ldots,c_{i-1},s(c_{i-1}),c_{i+1},\ldots,c_{k})

\displaystyle=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{i-1},r(c_{i+1}),c_{i+1},\ldots,c_{k})\in\mathcal{C}^{k-1}.

The identity morphism represented by any given instance of
\_
is determined by either of the neighbouring entries.

For each
k\geq 1
and
0\leq i\leq k
we define the
degeneracy map

\sigma_{k}^{i}\colon C_{k}(\mathcal{C})\to C_{k+1}(\mathcal{C})
by

\sigma_{k}^{i}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{k-1}]=\begin{cases}[\_,c_{0},\ldots,c_{k-1}]&if $i=0$,\\
[c_{0},\ldots,c_{i-1},\_,c_{i},\ldots,c_{k-1}]&if $0<i<k$,\\
[c_{0},\ldots,c_{k-1},\_]&if $i=k$,\end{cases}

with
\sigma_{0}^{0}[x]=[x]
for
x\in\mathcal{C}^{0}
. These and the
\partial_{j}^{i}
satisfy the
simplicial identities
:

\displaystyle\partial_{k-1}^{i}\partial_{k}^{j}

\displaystyle=\partial^{j-1}_{k-1}\partial_{k}^{i}\quad\text{if }i<j

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}_{k+1}\sigma^{j}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{j+1}_{k+1}\sigma^{i}_{k}\quad\text{if }i\leq j,\text{ and}

\displaystyle\partial^{i}_{k}\sigma^{j}_{k}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sigma^{j-1}_{k}\partial_{k}^{i}&\text{if }i<j\\
\id_{C_{k}(\mathcal{C})}&\text{if }i=j\text{ or }i=j+1\\
\sigma^{j}_{k}\partial^{i-1}_{k}&\text{if }i>j+1,\end{cases}

so
(C_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}),\partial,\sigma)
is a simplicial abelian group.

If
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
is a matched pair, then Proposition
3.20
gives an action of
\mathcal{C}
on each
\mathcal{D}^{k+1}
. For
(c,d)\in\mathcal{C}*\mathcal{D}^{k+1}
, we write
c\triangleright[d]
for the generator
[c\triangleright d]
of
C_{k+1}(\mathcal{D})
. Similarly if
(c,d)\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1}*\mathcal{D}
we write
[c]\triangleleft d
for the generator
[c\triangleleft d]
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
be a matched pair and take
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For
0<i\leq k+1
and for

(c,d)\in\mathcal{C}*\mathcal{D}^{k+1}
, we have
\partial^{i}_{k}(c\triangleright[d])=c\triangleright\partial^{i}_{k}[d]
in
C_{k}(\mathcal{D})
. Similarly, for
0\leq j<k+1\in\mathbb{N}
and
(c,d)\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1}*\mathcal{D}
, we have
\partial^{j}_{k}([c]\triangleleft d)=\partial^{j}_{k}[c]\triangleleft d
in
C_{k}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

We prove the first statement; the second follows symmetrically. Since
i>0
, we have

\displaystyle c\triangleright\partial_{k}^{i}[d]

\displaystyle=c\triangleright[d_{0},\dots,d_{i}d_{i+1},\dots,d_{k}]

\displaystyle=\big[c\triangleright d_{0},(c\triangleleft d_{0})\triangleright d_{1},\dots,(c\triangleleft d_{0}\dots d_{i-1})\triangleright d_{i}d_{i+1},\dots,(c\triangleleft(d_{0}\dots d_{k-1}))\triangleright d_{k}\big]

\displaystyle=\big[c\triangleright d_{0},(c\triangleleft d_{0})\triangleright d_{1},\dots,\big((c\triangleleft d_{0}\dots d_{i-1})\triangleright d_{i}\big)\big((c\triangleleft d_{0}\dots d_{i})\triangleright d_{i+1}\big),

\displaystyle\hskip 120.00018pt\dots,(c\triangleleft(d_{0}\dots d_{k-1}))\triangleright d_{k}\big]

\displaystyle=\partial_{k}^{i}\big[c\triangleright d_{0},(c\triangleleft d_{0})\triangleright d_{1},\dots,\big((c\triangleleft d_{0}\dots d_{i-1})\triangleright d_{i}),\big((c\triangleleft d_{0}\dots d_{i})\triangleright d_{i+1}\big),

\displaystyle\hskip 120.00018pt\dots,(c\triangleleft(d_{0}\dots d_{k-1}))\triangleright d_{k}\big]

\displaystyle=\partial_{k}^{i}(c\triangleright[d]).\qed

Remark 4.5
.

Lemma
4.4
is
only
valid for
i>0
and
j<k+1
. The left action of
\mathcal{C}
on
\mathcal{D}^{k+1}
does not commute with
\partial^{0}_{k}
, and the right action of
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}
does not commute with
\partial^{k+1}_{k}
.

4.2.
The matched complex

We associate a double complex to each matched pair
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
. For
p,\,q\geq 0
, regard elements of
\mathcal{C}^{p}*\mathcal{D}^{q}
as composable tuples in
(\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D})^{p+q}
, whose first
p
terms belong to
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D}
and whose remaining
q
terms belong to
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D}
.

Let
C_{p,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\coloneqq\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{C}^{p}*\mathcal{D}^{q})
, the free abelian group generated by
\mathcal{C}^{p}*\mathcal{D}^{q}
. Let
\triangleright
be the action of
\mathcal{C}
on
\mathcal{D}^{q}
of Lemma
3.19
. Define
horizontal face maps

\partial_{p,q}^{h,i}\colon C_{p+1,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\to C_{p,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
as follows. For
q\geq 1
,

\partial_{p,q}^{h,i}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{p},d_{0},\ldots,d_{q-1}]=\begin{cases}[c_{1},\ldots,c_{p},d_{0},\ldots,d_{q-1}]&\text{if }i=0\\
[c_{0},\ldots,c_{i-1}c_{i},\ldots,c_{p},d_{0},\ldots,d_{q-1}]&\text{if }1\leq i\leq p\\
[c_{0},\ldots,c_{p-1},c_{p}\triangleright(d_{0},\ldots,d_{q-1})]&\text{if }i=p+1,\end{cases}

while
\partial_{p,0}^{h,i}\coloneqq\partial_{p}^{i}\colon C_{p+1}(\mathcal{C})\to C_{p}(\mathcal{C})
as in (
4.1
). For
0\leq i\leq p
we define the

horizontal degeneracy maps

\sigma_{p,q}^{h,i}\colon C_{p,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\to C_{p+1,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
by

\sigma^{h,i}_{p,q}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{p-1},d_{0},\ldots,d_{q-1}]=[c_{0},\ldots,c_{i},\_,c_{i+1},\ldots,c_{p-1},d_{0},\ldots,d_{q-1}].

For each
q\geq 0
, the tuple
(C_{\bullet,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}),\partial^{h}_{\bullet,q},\sigma^{h}_{\bullet,q})
is a simplicial abelian group.

Let
\triangleleft
be the action of
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathcal{C}^{*}
of Lemma
3.19
. Define
vertical face maps

\partial_{p,q+1}^{v,j}\colon C_{p,q+1}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\to C_{p,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
as follows. For
p>0
,

\partial_{p,q}^{v,j}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{p-1},d_{0},\ldots,d_{q}]=(-1)^{p}\begin{cases}[(c_{0},\ldots,c_{p-1})\triangleleft d_{0},d_{1},\ldots,d_{q}]&\text{if }j=0\\
[c_{0},\ldots,c_{p-1},d_{0},\ldots,d_{j-1}d_{j},\ldots,d_{q}]&\text{if }1\leq j\leq q\\
[c_{0},\ldots,c_{p-1},d_{0},\ldots,d_{q-1}]&\text{if }j=q+1,\end{cases}

while
\partial_{0,q}^{v,i}\coloneqq\partial_{q}^{i}\colon C_{q+1}(\mathcal{D})\to C_{q}(\mathcal{D})
as in (
4.1
). For
0\leq j\leq q
we define
vertical degeneracy maps

\sigma_{p,q}^{v,j}\colon C_{p,q}\to C_{p,q+1}
by

\sigma^{v,j}_{p,q}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{p-1},d_{0},\ldots,d_{q-1}]=(-1)^{p}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{p-1},d_{0},\ldots,d_{j},\_,d_{j+1},d_{q-1}].

Then
(C_{p,\bullet}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}),\partial^{v}_{p,\bullet},\sigma^{v}_{p,\bullet})
is also a simplicial abelian group.

For the next result, recall from
[
Wei94
, §8.5]
that a bisimplicial abelian group is a quintuple
(C_{\bullet,\bullet},\partial^{h},\partial^{v},\sigma^{h},\sigma^{v})
consisting of abelian groups
C_{p,q}
and homomorphisms
\partial^{h,i}_{p,q}\colon C_{p+1,q}\to C_{p,q}
,
\sigma^{h,i}_{p,q}\colon C_{p,q}\to C_{p+1,q}
,
\partial^{v,j}_{p,q}\colon C_{p,q+1}\to C_{p,q}
, and
\sigma^{v,j}_{p,q}\colon C_{p,q}\to C_{p,q+1}
such that each
(C_{p,\bullet},\partial^{v}_{p,\bullet},\sigma^{v}_{p,\bullet})
and each
(C_{\bullet,q},\partial^{h}_{\bullet,q},\sigma^{h}_{\bullet,q})
is a simplicial group, and

\partial_{p,q}^{v,i}\partial_{p,q+1}^{h,j}=-\partial_{p,q}^{h,j}\partial_{p+1,q}^{v,i}\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{p+1,q}^{v,i}\sigma_{p,q}^{h,j}=-\sigma_{p,q+1}^{h,j}\sigma_{p,q}^{v,i}.

Proposition 4.6
.

The quintuple
(C_{\bullet,\bullet},\partial^{h},\partial^{v},\sigma^{h},\sigma^{v})
is a bisimplicial group. Define

d_{p,q}^{h}=\sum_{i=0}^{p+1}(-1)^{i}\partial_{p,q}^{h,i}\colon C_{p+1,q}\to C_{p,q}\quad\text{and}\quad d_{p,q}^{v}=\sum_{i=0}^{q+1}(-1)^{i}\partial_{p,q}^{v,i}\colon C_{p,q+1}\to C_{p,q}.

Then

(4.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{0,2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1,2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{2,2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{0,1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1,1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{2,1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{0,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{2,0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{v}_{0,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{v}_{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{h}_{0,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{v}_{2,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{h}_{1,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{v}_{0,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{v}_{1,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{h}_{0,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{v}_{2,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{h}_{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{h}_{0,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{h}_{1,0}}

is a first-quadrant double chain complex satisfying
d^{h}d^{v}=-d^{v}d^{h}
.

Proof.

Fix
p,q\geq 0
and fix
i\leq p+1
and
j\leq q+1
. We must show that
\partial_{p,q}^{v,i}\partial_{p,q+1}^{h,j}=-\partial_{p,q}^{h,j}\partial_{p+1,q}^{v,i}
. Fix
[c_{0},\dots,c_{p};d_{0},\dots,d_{q}]\in C_{p+1,q+1}
. If
i\not=p+1
or
j\not=0
, then
\partial_{p,q}^{v,i}
and
\partial_{p,q+1}^{h,j}
concatenate or delete nonadjacent coordinates, as do
\partial_{p,q}^{h,j}
and
\partial_{p+1,q}^{v,i}
, and so the factors of
(-1)^{p}
and
(-1)^{p+1}
in
\partial_{p,q}^{v,i}
and
\partial_{p+1,q}^{v,i}
give the desired anticommutation relation. If
i=p+1
and
j=0
, then

\displaystyle\partial_{p,q}^{v,0}\partial_{p,q+1}^{h,p+1}([c_{0},\dots,c_{p};d_{0},\dots,d_{q}])

\displaystyle=\partial_{p,q}^{v,0}([(c_{0},\dots,c_{p})\triangleleft d_{0};d_{1},\dots,d_{q}])

\displaystyle=\partial_{p,q}^{v,0}([(c_{0},\dots,c_{p-1})\triangleleft(c_{p}\triangleright d_{0}),c_{p}\triangleleft d_{0};d_{1},\dots,d_{q}])

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p}[(c_{0},\dots,c_{p-1})\triangleleft(c_{p}\triangleright d_{0});(c_{p}\triangleleft d_{0})\triangleright(d_{1},\dots,d_{q})],

and

\displaystyle\partial_{p,q}^{h,p+1}\partial_{p+1,q}^{v,0}([c_{0},\dots,c_{p};d_{0},\dots,d_{q}])

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p+1}\partial_{p,q}^{v,0}([c_{0},\dots,c_{p-1};c_{p}\triangleright(d_{0},\dots,d_{q})])

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p+1}\partial_{p,q}^{v,0}([c_{0},\dots,c_{p-1};c_{p}\triangleright d_{0},(c_{p}\triangleleft d_{0})\triangleright(d_{1},\dots,d_{q})])

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p+1}[(c_{0},\dots,c_{p-1})\triangleleft(c_{p}\triangleright d_{0});(c_{p}\triangleleft d_{0})\triangleright(d_{1},\dots,d_{q})],

which gives the desired relation. It follows that
d^{v}d^{h}=-d^{h}d^{v}
.

The anticommutation relation
\sigma_{p+1,q}^{v,i}\sigma_{p,q}^{h,j}=-\sigma_{p,q+1}^{h,j}\sigma_{p,q}^{v,i}
also
follows from direct computation. Routine calculation shows that (
4.2
) is a first-quadrant double chain complex.
∎

Definition 4.7
.

We call the double chain complex
(C_{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}),d^{h},d^{v})
the
matched complex
of the matched pair
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
.

Lemma 4.8
.

The assignment
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
of a matched complex to each matched pair is a functor from the category
{\mathsf{MP}}
of matched pairs to the category of double complexes of Abelian groups.

Proof.

Matched-pair morphisms intertwine the face and degeneracy maps
\partial_{p,q}^{\bullet,i}
and
\sigma_{p,q}^{\bullet,i}
.
∎

Notation 4.9
.

For the remainder of the paper we frequently omit the subscripts on face maps, degeneracy maps and boundary maps. For example,
\partial^{h,i}
denotes any of the maps
\partial^{h,i}_{p,q}
; the values of
p
and
q
should be clear from context.

There are two chain complexes associated to each double complex: the
diagonal complex
and the
total complex

[
Wei94
, §8.5]
.

4.3.
The diagonal complex and diagonal homology

Let
(C_{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}),d^{h},d^{v})
be the matched complex of a matched pair
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
. For each
k\geq 0
, let

C_{k}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\coloneqq C_{k,k}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})

and define
\partial^{\Delta,i}_{k}\colon C_{k+1}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\to C_{k}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
and
\sigma^{\Delta,i}_{k}\colon C_{k}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\to C_{k+1}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
by

\partial^{\Delta,i}_{k}\coloneqq\partial^{h,i}_{k,k}\partial^{v,i}_{k+1,k}\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma^{\Delta,i}_{k}\coloneqq\sigma^{v,i}_{k+1,k}\sigma^{h,i}_{k,k}.

Then
(C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}),\partial^{\Delta},\sigma^{\Delta})
is a simplicial group
[
Wei94
, §8.5]
. Let
d^{\Delta}_{k}\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{k+1}(-1)^{i}\partial^{\Delta,i}_{k}
.

Definition 4.10
.

The
diagonal complex
of
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
is the chain complex
(C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}),d^{\Delta}_{\bullet})
. We denote the homology of this chain complex by
H^{\Delta}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
.

4.4.
The total complex and total homology

Let
(C_{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}),d^{h},d^{v})
be the matched complex of a matched pair
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
. For each
k\geq 0
, let

C_{k}^{\Tot}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\coloneqq\bigoplus_{p+q=k}C_{p,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}).

Define
d^{\Tot}_{k}\colon C_{k+1}^{\Tot}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\to C_{k}^{\Tot}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
by
d^{\Tot}_{k}\coloneqq d_{k}^{v}+d_{k}^{h}
.

Definition 4.11
.

The
total complex
of
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
is the chain complex
(C_{\bullet}^{\Tot}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}),d^{\Tot}_{\bullet})
. We denote the homology of this complex by
H^{\Tot}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
.

5.
Equivalence of homology theories

In this section we prove that the homology theories for matched pairs introduced in Subsections
4.1
,
4.3
, and
4.4
coincide. Specifically, we describe natural chain maps that induce isomorphisms between them and between the dual cohomology theories. We also give formulae for their inverses. The main result is Theorem
5.3
. We start by defining the maps involved.

5.1.
The natural chain maps

We begin by describing explicit formulae for natural chain maps
\nabla\colon C_{\bullet}^{\Tot}\to C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}
,
\Pi\colon C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}\to C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}
, and
\Psi\colon C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}\to C_{\bullet}^{\Tot}
.

5.1.1.
The map
\nabla

The map
\nabla\colon C_{\bullet}^{\Tot}\to C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}
is the
Eilenberg–Zilber map

[
Wei94
, § 8.5.4]
.
For
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
a
(p,q)
-shuffle
is a permutation
\beta
of
\{1,\ldots,p+q\}
such that

\beta(1)<\beta(2)<\cdots<\beta(p)\quad\text{ and }\quad\beta(p+1)<\beta(p+2)<\cdots<\beta(p+q).

We write
\Sh(p,q)
for the collection of all
(p,q)
-shuffles,
and
\sgn(\beta)
for the sign of a permutation
\beta
.
The
(p,q)
-component
\nabla_{p,q}\colon C_{p,q}\to C_{p+q}^{\Delta}
of the Eilenberg–Zilber map is

(5.1)

\nabla_{p,q}=\sum_{\beta\in\Sh(p,q)}\sgn(\beta)\,\sigma^{h,\beta(p+q)}_{p+q-1,p+q}\circ\cdots\circ\sigma^{h,\beta(p+1)}_{p,p+q}\circ\sigma^{v,\beta(p)}_{p,p+q-1}\circ\cdots\circ\sigma^{v,\beta(1)}_{p,q}.

5.1.2.
The map
\Pi

We describe
\Pi\colon C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}\to C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}
. For
k\geq 1
define
\bowtie^{k}\colon(\mathcal{C}*\mathcal{D})^{k}\to(\mathcal{D}*\mathcal{C})^{k}
by

\bowtie^{k}(c_{1},d_{1},\ldots,c_{k},d_{k})\coloneqq(c_{1}\bowtie d_{1},\ldots,c_{k}\bowtie d_{k}).

Set
\Pi_{0}=\id_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}
and inductively define
\Pi_{k}\colon\mathcal{C}^{k}*\mathcal{D}^{k}\to(\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D})^{k}=(\mathcal{D}*\mathcal{C})^{k}
for
k\geq 1
by

(5.2)

\Pi_{k}\coloneqq\bowtiemap^{k}\circ(1_{\mathcal{C}}*\Pi_{k-1}*1_{\mathcal{D}}).

These extend to homomorphisms
\Pi_{k}\colon C^{\Delta}_{k}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\to C^{\bowtie}_{k}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
. For example,

\displaystyle\Pi_{1}[c_{1},d_{1}]

\displaystyle=[c_{1}\triangleright d_{1},c_{1}\triangleleft d_{1}]

\displaystyle\Pi_{2}[c_{1},c_{2},d_{1},d_{2}]

\displaystyle=[c_{1}c_{2}\triangleright d_{1},c_{1}\triangleleft(c_{2}\triangleright d_{1}),(c_{2}\triangleleft d_{1})\triangleright d_{2},c_{2}\triangleleft d_{1}d_{2}]

\displaystyle\Pi_{3}[c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}]

\displaystyle=[c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}\triangleright d_{1},c_{1}\triangleleft(c_{2}c_{3}\triangleright d_{1}),(c_{2}c_{3}\triangleleft d_{1})\triangleright d_{2},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad c_{2}\triangleleft(c_{3}\triangleright d_{1}d_{2}),(c_{3}\triangleleft d_{1}d_{2})\triangleright d_{3},c_{3}\triangleleft d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}].

An induction on
k
, using that matched-pair morphisms respect left and right actions, shows that the
\Pi_{k}
extend to natural transformations
\Pi_{k}\colon C_{k}^{\Delta}\to C_{k}^{\bowtie}
.

Remark 5.1
.

The map
\Pi_{k}
can be described diagrammatically. We represent elements of
\mathcal{C}
by blue vertices, and elements of
\mathcal{D}
by red vertices; vertical lines are identity morphisms; and crossings are applications of
\bowtie
:

\displaystyle[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{3}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{1}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{2}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{3}}]

\displaystyle[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{2}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}c_{3}\triangleright d_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{3}\triangleleft d_{1}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{2}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{3}}]

\displaystyle[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{1}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}c_{2}c_{3}\triangleright d_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{2}\triangleleft(c_{3}\triangleright d_{1})},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(c_{3}\triangleleft d_{1})\triangleright d_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{3}\triangleleft d_{1}d_{2}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{3}}]

\displaystyle\Pi_{3}[c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}].

So starting with an element of
\mathcal{C}^{k}*\mathcal{D}^{k}
, we apply
\bowtie
to pairs of adjacent terms wherever possible until we obtain an element of
(\mathcal{D}*\mathcal{C})^{k}
.

5.1.3.
The map
\Psi

We now define
\Psi\colon C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}\to C_{\bullet}^{\Tot}
.
For
q\geq 0
define

\tau^{q}\colon(\mathcal{D}\bowtie\mathcal{C})^{q}\to\mathcal{D}^{q}*\mathcal{C}^{q}

as follows: regard
(d_{1}c_{1},\ldots,d_{q}c_{q})\in(\mathcal{D}\bowtie\mathcal{C})^{q}
as a composable
q
-tuple in
\mathcal{C}^{*}\bowtie\mathcal{D}^{*}
. By Proposition
3.20
there exist unique

d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
and
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{q}
such that
(d_{1}c_{1})\cdots(d_{q}c_{q})=d^{\prime}c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{p}\bowtie\mathcal{D}^{q}
. For instance,

(d_{1}c_{1})(d_{2}c_{2})(d_{3}c_{3})=(d_{1},c_{1}\triangleright d_{2},((c_{1}\triangleleft d_{2})c_{2})\triangleright d_{3},c_{1}\triangleleft(d_{2}(c_{2}\triangleright d_{3})),c_{2}\triangleleft d_{3},c_{3})\in\mathcal{D}^{3}*\mathcal{C}^{3}.

We define
\tau^{q}(d_{1}c_{1},\ldots,d_{q}c_{q})\coloneqq(d^{\prime},c^{\prime})
.

Remark 5.2
.

We can describe
\tau^{q}
via a diagram using the same conventions as in Remark
5.1
. For example
\tau^{3}
is represented by the diagram

\displaystyle({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{1}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{2}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{3}})

\displaystyle({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{1}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}c_{1}\triangleright d_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{1}\triangleleft d_{2}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}c_{2}\triangleright d_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{2}\triangleleft d_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{3}})

\displaystyle({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{1}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}c_{1}\triangleright d_{2}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}((c_{1}\triangleleft d_{2})c_{2})\triangleright d_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{1}\triangleleft(d_{2}(c_{2}\triangleright d_{3}))},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{2}\triangleleft d_{3}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{3}})

The maps
\tau^{q}
for
q\geq 3
can be visualised similarly.

For
p,q\geq 0
let
\rho_{p,q}\colon\mathcal{D}^{p}*\mathcal{C}^{p}*\mathcal{D}^{q}*\mathcal{C}^{q}\to\mathcal{C}^{p}*\mathcal{D}^{q}
denote the projection onto the middle two factors. Define
\Psi_{p,q}\colon(\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D})^{p+q}\to\mathcal{C}^{p}*\mathcal{D}^{q}
by

\Psi_{p,q}=\rho_{p,q}\circ(\tau_{p}*\tau_{q})

and extend it to a homomorphism
\Psi_{p,q}\colon C_{p+q}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\to C_{p,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
. For example, we can represent
\Psi_{3,2}[d_{1},c_{1},\ldots,d_{5},c_{5}]
diagrammatically by
[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{1}},\dots,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{5}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{5}}]
(\tau^{3}*\tau^{2})([{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{1}},\dots,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{5}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{5}}])
\Psi_{3,2}([{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{1}},\dots,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{5}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c_{5}}])

(crossed vertices like

indicate omission of the corresponding entries).

We now define

\Psi_{k}\colon C_{k}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\to C_{k}^{\Tot}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
by

(5.3)

\Psi_{k}=\sum_{p+q=k}\Psi_{p,q}.

Explicit formulae for low-degree terms are given by

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}[d_{1}c_{1}]

\displaystyle=\Psi_{1,0}[d_{1}c_{1}]+\Psi_{0,1}[d_{1}c_{1}]=[c_{1}]+[d_{1}]

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}[d_{1}c_{1},d_{2}c_{2}]

\displaystyle=[c_{1}\triangleleft d_{2},c_{2}]+[c_{1};d_{2}]+[d_{1},c_{1}\triangleright d_{2}]

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}[d_{1}c_{1},d_{2}c_{2},d_{3}c_{3}]

\displaystyle=[d_{1},c_{1}\triangleright d_{2},((c_{1}\triangleleft d_{2})c_{2})\triangleright d_{3}]+[c_{1};d_{2},c_{2}\triangleright d_{3}]

\displaystyle\qquad+[c_{1}\triangleleft d_{2},c_{2};d_{3}]+[c_{1}\triangleleft(d_{2}(c_{2}\triangleright d_{3})),c_{2}\triangleleft d_{3},c_{3}].

It is routine to verify that the
\Psi_{k}
extend to natural transformations
\Psi_{k}\colon C_{k}^{\bowtie}\to C_{k}^{\Tot}
.

5.2.
The statement of the main theorem

We state our main homology theorem and outline the proof.
We write
{\mathsf{Ch}}_{\bullet}
for the category of abelian chain complexes and chain maps.

Theorem 5.3
.

The formulae (
5.1
), (
5.2
), and (
5.3
) determine natural chain equivalences such that the diagram

(5.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{\bullet}^{\Tot}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

commutes up to natural chain homotopy. They induce natural isomorphisms

H^{\bowtie}_{\bullet}\cong H^{\Delta}_{\bullet}\cong H^{\Tot}_{\bullet}

of functors from
{\mathsf{MP}}
to
{\mathsf{Ch}}_{\bullet}
.
In particular, for any matched pair
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
,

H^{\bowtie}_{k}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\cong H^{\Delta}_{k}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\cong H^{\Tot}_{k}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}).

Before commencing the proof of this theorem, we record a corollary.
Recall that for us, given a chain complex
(C_{\bullet},d_{\bullet})
and an abelian group
A
, the cohomology with coefficients in
A
is the cohomology of the dual cochain complex
(\Hom(C_{\bullet},A),d_{\bullet}^{*})
.

Corollary 5.4
.

For any fixed abelian group
A
, the duals of the natural chain maps
\nabla
,
\Pi
, and
\Psi
induce natural isomorphisms

H^{\bullet}_{\bowtie}(\,\cdot\,;A)\cong H^{\bullet}_{\Delta}(\,\cdot\,;A)\cong H^{\bullet}_{\Tot}(\,\cdot\,;A)

of cohomology functors with coefficients in
A
.

Proof.

Dualising all the maps in a chain homotopy diagram yields a cochain homotopy.
∎

That
\nabla
induces a natural isomorphism is the content of a general form of the Eilenberg–Zilber Theorem. Following
[
Wei94
, §8.5.4]
the
Alexander–Whitney map

\Delta\colon C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}\to C_{\bullet}^{\Tot}
is defined as follows: for
p,q
such that
p+q=n
, define
\Delta_{p,q}\colon C_{n}^{\Delta}\to C_{p,q}
by

\Delta_{p,q}\coloneqq\underbrace{\partial_{p+1}^{h}\cdots\partial_{n}^{h}}_{q\text{ terms}}\circ\underbrace{\partial_{0}^{v}\cdots\partial_{0}^{v}}_{p\text{ terms}}.

Then the map
\Delta
is defined by

(5.5)

\Delta\coloneqq\bigoplus_{p+q=n}\Delta_{p,q}.

Theorem 5.5

(
[
Wei94
, Theorem 8.5.1]
)
.

The map
\nabla\colon C_{\bullet}^{\Tot}\to C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}
of (
5.1
) induces a natural isomorphism
H^{\Delta}_{\bullet}\cong H^{\Tot}_{\bullet}
, with inverse induced by the map
\Delta\colon C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}\to C_{\bullet}^{\Tot}
of (
5.5
).

Given Theorem
5.5
, to prove Theorem
5.3
it suffices to establish the natural isomorphism
H^{\bowtie}_{\bullet}\cong H^{\Delta}_{\bullet}
. To do this we fill out a diagram

(5.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{\bullet}^{\Tot}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi}

\scriptstyle\Pi

\scriptstyle\Psi

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

of natural chain equivalences that commutes up to natural chain homotopy. We use the
method of acyclic models
(see
[
Rot88
]
for instance).
The details occupy Subsections
5.3
and
5.4
.

We show in Section
5.3
that the model matched pairs
(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})

satisfy
H_{p}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})=0=H_{p}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})
for all
p\geq 1
. So we can use these as the models in the method of acyclic models. We deduce that there exist natural chain equivalences between
C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}
and
C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}
that induce natural isomorphisms on homology, and show how to recognise when given chain maps do the job.

In Subsection
5.5
, we show that (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) are such chain maps. We also
give explicit formulae for the remaining maps

\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Pi$}\coloneqq\nabla\circ\Psi
and
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Psi$}\coloneqq\Pi\circ\nabla
.

5.3.
Homological acyclicity of model matched pairs

The proof of Theorem
5.3
hinges on properties of the homology of the model matched pairs
(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})
. Recall that the object set of
\Gamma_{n}\cong\mathcal{E}_{n}*\mathcal{F}_{n}
is
X_{n}=\{\mathbf{a}=(a_{L},a_{R})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\mid 0\leq a_{L}+a_{R}\leq n\}
. Each morphism of
\Gamma_{n}
is a pair
(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})\in X_{n}\times X_{n}
such that
a_{L}\leq b_{L}
and
a_{R}\geq b_{R}
.

The map

r\times s\colon\Gamma_{n}\to X_{n}\times X_{n}
is injective. Hence,

(5.7)

\Gamma_{n}^{k}\ni(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k})\mapsto(r(\gamma_{1}),s(\gamma_{1}),s(\gamma_{2}),\ldots,s(\gamma_{k}))\in X_{n}^{(k)}.

is a bijective correspondence between
\Gamma_{n}^{k}
and

X_{n}^{(k)}\coloneqq\{(\mathbf{a}_{0},\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{k})\mid\mathbf{a}_{i}\in X_{n},\,a_{i,L}\leq a_{i+1,L},\text{ and }a_{i,R}\geq a_{i+1,R}\text{ for all }i\}.

Since
C^{\bowtie}_{k}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})
is the free group
\mathbb{Z}\Gamma_{n}^{k}
, we have
C^{\bowtie}_{k}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}X_{n}^{(k)}.

Let
\langle\mathbf{a}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{k}\rangle
denote the generator of
\mathbb{Z}X_{n}^{(k)}
corresponding to
(\mathbf{a}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{k})\in X_{n}^{(k)}
. Using carats to denote elision of coordinates, the face and degeneracy maps on
C_{k}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})
are

\partial^{\bowtie,i}_{k}\langle\mathbf{a}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{k}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{a}_{0},\ldots,\widehat{\mathbf{a}}_{i},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{k}\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma^{\bowtie,i}_{k}\langle\mathbf{a}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{k}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{a}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{k}\rangle.

A chain complex
(C_{\bullet},d_{\bullet})
is
acyclic
if
H_{0}(C_{\bullet},d_{\bullet})\cong\mathbb{Z}
and
H_{k}(C_{\bullet},d_{\bullet})=0
for
k\geq 1
.

Recall that an
initial object
in a category
\mathcal{C}
is an object
v\in\mathcal{C}^{0}
such that
w\mathcal{C}v
has precisely one element for each
w\in\mathcal{C}^{0}
.

The following is well-known, but could not find an explicit reference.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small category with an initial object
v
. Let
\mathbbm{1}
be the category with a single morphism
1
. Let
\iota\colon\mathbbm{1}\to\mathcal{C}
be the functor such that
\iota(1)=v
. Let
\rho
be the unique functor from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathbbm{1}
. Then
\rho\circ\iota=\id_{\mathbbm{1}}
, and
(\iota\circ\rho)_{\bullet}\colon C_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})\to C_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})
is chain-homotopic to
\id_{C_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})}
. In particular,
(C_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}),d_{\bullet})
is acyclic.

Proof.

Clearly,
\rho\circ\iota=\id_{\mathbbm{1}}
.
For each
w\in\mathcal{C}^{0}
let
\tau_{w}\in\mathcal{C}
be the unique morphism from
v
to
w
.
Fix
k\geq 0
. For
0\leq i\leq k
define
h^{i}\colon C_{k}(\mathcal{C})\to C_{k+1}(\mathcal{C})
by

h^{i}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{k-1}]=\begin{cases}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{i},\tau_{s(c_{i})},v,\ldots,v]&if $i>0$\\
[\tau_{r(c_{0})},v,\ldots,v]&if $i=0$.\end{cases}

To see that
h
is a simplicial homotopy we need to check that
\partial^{0}h^{0}=(\iota\circ\rho)_{k}
, that
\partial^{k+1}h^{k}=\id_{C_{k}(\mathcal{C})}
, that
\partial^{i}h^{j}=h^{j-1}\partial^{i}
for
i<j
, that
\partial^{i}h^{j}=h^{j}\partial^{i-1}
for
i>j+1
, that
\sigma^{i}h^{j}=h^{j+1}\sigma^{i}
for
i\leq j
, and that
\sigma^{i}h^{j}=h^{j}\sigma^{i-1}
for
i>j
. For the first two identities, we calculate

\displaystyle\partial^{0}h^{0}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{k-1}]

\displaystyle=\partial^{0}[\tau_{r(c_{0})},v,\ldots,v]=[v,\ldots,v],\text{ and}

\displaystyle\partial^{k+1}h^{k}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{k-1}]

\displaystyle=\partial^{k+1}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{k-1},\tau_{s(c_{k-1})}]=[c_{0},\ldots,c_{k-1}].

The remaining four conditions follow from similar calculations. For example, if
0<i<j
, then

\displaystyle\partial^{i}h^{j}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{k-1}]

\displaystyle=\partial^{i}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{j},\tau_{s(c_{j})},v,\ldots,v]=[c_{0},\ldots,c_{i-1}c_{i},\ldots,c_{j},\tau_{s(c_{j})},v,\ldots,v]

\displaystyle=h^{j-1}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{i-1}c_{i},\ldots,c_{j},\ldots,c_{k-1}]=h^{j-1}\partial^{i}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{k-1}].

Hence, the simplicial maps
(\iota\circ p)_{\bullet}
and
\id_{C_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})}
are simplicially homotopic. So

s_{k}\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}h_{k}^{i}

defines a chain homotopy
s
between
(\iota\circ p)_{\bullet}
and
\id_{C_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C})}

[
Wei94
, Lemma 8.3.13]
.

The final statement follows from acyclicity of
(C_{\bullet}(\mathbbm{1}),d_{\bullet})
.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

For each
n\geq 0
the chain complex
(C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n}),d)
is acyclic.

Proof.

The object
(n,0)
is an initial object in
\Gamma_{n}=\mathcal{E}_{n}\bowtie\mathcal{F}_{n}
, so the result follows from Lemma
5.6
.
∎

Chains in
C_{k,l}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})
also admit a tractable description. The formula

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{n}^{k}*\mathcal{F}_{n}^{l}\ni

\displaystyle((p_{0},q_{0}),(p_{1},q_{0}),\ldots,(p_{k},q_{0}),\ldots,(p_{k},q_{l-1}),(p_{k},q_{l}))

\displaystyle\quad\mapsto(p_{0},p_{1},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{l-1},q_{l})\in Y_{n}^{(k,l)}

is a bijection between
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{k}*\mathcal{F}_{n}^{l}
and

Y_{n}^{(k,l)}\coloneqq\Big\{(p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{l})\in\mathbb{N}^{2k}\mid p_{i}\leq p_{i+1},\,q_{i}\geq q_{i+1},\text{ and }p_{k}+q_{0}\leq n\Big\},

and induces an isomorphism

C_{k,l}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}Y_{n}^{(k,l)}.

We write
\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{l}\rangle
for the generator of
\mathbb{Z}Y_{n}^{(k,l)}
that corresponds to the tuple
(p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{l})\in Y_{n}^{(k,l)}
. The face maps in the double complex become

\displaystyle\partial^{h,i}\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{l}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle p_{0},\ldots,\widehat{p_{i}},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{l}\rangle\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\partial^{v,i}\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{l}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,\widehat{q_{i}},\ldots,q_{l}\rangle.

The degeneracy maps become

\displaystyle\sigma^{h,i}\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{l}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{i-1},p_{i},p_{i},p_{i+1},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{l}\rangle\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\sigma^{v,i}\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{l}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{i-1},q_{i},q_{i},q_{i+1},\ldots,q_{l}\rangle.

In particular, for the diagonal complex
C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})
the face and degeneracy maps are

\displaystyle\partial^{\Delta,i}\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{k}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle p_{0},\ldots,\widehat{p_{i}},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,\widehat{q_{i}},\ldots,q_{k}\rangle\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\sigma^{\Delta,i}\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{k}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{i-1},p_{i},p_{i},p_{i+1},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{i-1},q_{i},q_{i},q_{i+1},\ldots,q_{k}\rangle.

Lemma 5.8
.

The diagonal complex
(C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n}),d^{\Delta})
is acyclic.

Proof.

Consider the directed graph

G_{n}=0\overset{e_{0}}{\leftarrow}1\overset{e_{1}}{\leftarrow}\cdots\overset{e_{n-1}}{\leftarrow}n
. Since
n
is an initial object for
G_{n}^{*}
, Lemma
5.6
implies that
(C_{\bullet}(G_{n}^{*}),d_{\bullet})
is acyclic. So it suffices to show that
(C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n}),d^{\Delta})
is chain-homotopic to
(C_{\bullet}(G_{n}^{*}),d)
.

The group
C_{k}(G_{n}^{*})
is freely generated by
k
-tuples
\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k}\rangle
where
0\leq p_{i}\leq p_{i+1}\leq n
for each
0\leq i<k
.
The functor
\iota\colon G_{n}^{*}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{n}
given by
\iota(e_{p})=e_{p,0}
induces a chain map
\iota\colon C_{\bullet}(G_{n}^{*})\to C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})
satisfying

\iota_{k}\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k}\rangle=\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};0,\ldots,0\rangle.

The functor
\rho\colon\mathcal{E}_{n}\to G_{n}^{*}
defined by
\rho(e_{p,q})=e_{p}
, induces a chain map
\rho\colon C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})\to C_{\bullet}(G_{n}^{*})
satisfying

\rho_{k}\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{k}\rangle=\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k}\rangle.

We have
\rho_{k}\circ\iota_{k}=\id_{C_{k}(G_{n}^{*})}
. For
0\leq i\leq k
define
h^{i}\colon C_{k}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})\to C_{k+1}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})
by

h^{i}\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{k}\rangle=\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{i-1},p_{i},p_{i},p_{i+1},\ldots,p_{k};0,\ldots,0,q_{i},q_{i+1},\ldots,q_{k}\rangle.

Direct calculation shows that
\partial^{0}h^{0}=\id_{C_{k}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})}
and
\partial^{k+1}h^{k}=(\iota\circ\rho)_{k}
.

It is routine to check that
\partial^{i}h^{j}=h^{j-1}\partial^{i}
for
i<j
and
\partial^{i}h^{j}=h^{j}\partial^{i-1}
for
i>j+1
. Similarly,
\sigma^{i}h^{j}=h^{j+1}\sigma^{i}
for
i\leq j
and
\sigma^{i}h^{j}=h^{j}\sigma^{i-1}
for
i>j
. It follows that the simplicial maps
(\iota\circ p)_{\bullet}
and
\id_{C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})}
are
simplicially homotopic.
∎

We identify some particularly useful chains in the categorical and diagonal homology of
(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})
.
For each
k\geq 0
define
x_{k}\in C_{k}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})
and
y_{k}\in C_{k}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})
by

(5.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}x_{k}&\coloneqq[f_{0,2k}e_{0,2k},f_{1,2k-1}e_{1,2k-1},\ldots,f_{k-1,k+1}e_{k-1,k+1}]=\langle(0,2k),(1,2k-1),\ldots,(k,k)\rangle\end{split}

and

(5.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}y_{k}&\coloneqq[e_{0,k},e_{1,k},\ldots,e_{k-1,k};f_{k,k-1},\ldots,f_{k,1},f_{k,0}]=\langle 0,1,\ldots,k-1,k;k,k-1,\ldots,1,0\rangle.\end{split}

Pictorially,
x_{k}
and
y_{k}
correspond to the following composable tuples in
\mathcal{E}_{2k}\bowtie\mathcal{F}_{2k}
:

x_{k}
(k,0)
(0,k)
(k,k)
(0,2k)

y_{k}
(k,0)
(0,k)
(k,k)
(0,2k)

By Corollary
3.10
, a matched pair morphism
(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n})\to(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
corresponds to a functor
\Gamma_{n}\to\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D}
taking
\mathcal{E}_{n}
to
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{F}_{n}
to
\mathcal{D}
. For each
\gamma=(d_{0}c_{0},\ldots,d_{k-1}c_{k-1})
in
(\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D})^{k}
, Lemma
3.25
gives a morphism
h_{\gamma}^{\bowtie}\colon\Gamma_{2k}\to\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D}
such that

h^{\bowtie}_{\gamma}(f_{p,2k-1-p}e_{p,2k-1-p})=\begin{cases}d_{p}c_{p}&\text{if }0\leq p<k\\
s(c_{k-1})&\text{if }k\leq p<2k.\end{cases}

For
\lambda=(c_{0},\ldots,c_{k-1},d_{0},\ldots,d_{k-1})\in\mathcal{C}^{k}*\mathcal{D}^{k}
, with
d^{\prime}_{i}=r(c_{i})
and
c^{\prime}_{i}=s(d_{i})
,
Lemma
3.25
applied to
(d^{\prime}_{0}c_{0},\cdots,d^{\prime}_{k-1}c_{k-1},d_{0}c^{\prime}_{0},\dots,d_{k-1}c^{\prime}_{k-1})
yields a morphism

h_{\lambda}^{\Delta}\colon\Gamma_{2k}\to\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D}
such that

h_{\lambda}^{\Delta}(f_{p,2k-1-p}e_{p,2k-1-p})=\begin{cases}c_{p}&\text{if }0\leq p<k\\
d_{p-k}&\text{if }k\leq p<2k.\end{cases}

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
be a matched pair. For
\gamma\in(\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D})^{k}
and
\lambda\in\mathcal{C}^{k}*\mathcal{D}^{k}
we have

[\gamma]=C^{\bowtie}_{k}(h^{\bowtie}_{\gamma})(x_{k})
and
[\lambda]=C^{\Delta}_{k}(h^{\Delta}_{\lambda})(y_{k})
.
Moreover,

\{C^{\bowtie}_{k}(h)(x_{k})\mid h\colon(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})\to(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\}\quad\text{ and }\quad\{C_{k}^{\Delta}(h)(y_{k})\mid h\colon(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})\to(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\}

generate
C^{\bowtie}_{k}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
and
C^{\Delta}_{k}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
respectively.

Proof.

That
[\gamma]=C^{\bowtie}_{k}(h^{\bowtie}_{\gamma})(x_{k})
and
[\lambda]=C^{\Delta}_{k}(h^{\Delta}_{\lambda})(y_{k})
follow immediately from the definitions of
h^{\bowtie}_{\gamma}
and
h^{\Delta}_{\lambda}
.
For the second statement, let
h\colon(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})\to(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
be a matched pair morphism. Then
C^{\bowtie}_{k}(h)(x_{k})=[h(f_{0,2k}e_{0,2k}),\ldots,h(f_{k-1,k+1}e_{k-1,k+1})]
.
So

\displaystyle\{C^{\bowtie}_{k}(h)(x_{k})\mid h\colon(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})\to(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\}\supseteq\{C^{\bowtie}_{k}(h^{\bowtie}_{\gamma})(x_{k})\mid\gamma\in(\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D})^{k}\}=\{[\gamma]\mid\gamma\in(\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D})^{k}\},

which generates
C_{k}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
.
Similarly,

\{C_{k}^{\Delta}(h)(y_{k})\mid h\colon(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})\to(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\}\supseteq\{[\lambda]\mid\lambda\in\mathcal{C}^{k}*\mathcal{D}^{k}\}

generates
C_{k}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
.
∎

In the terminology of
[
Rot88
, pp. 239–240]
, Lemma
5.9
says that the functors
C_{k}^{\bowtie}
and
C_{k}^{\Delta}
from
{\mathsf{MP}}
to
{\mathsf{Ch}}_{\bullet}
are
free
with bases
\{x_{k}\}
and
\{y_{k}\}
, giving the following lemma.

Lemma 5.10

(
[
Rot88
, Lemma 9.10]
)
.

If
G\colon{\mathsf{MP}}\to{\mathsf{Ab}}
is a functor and
g\in G(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})
, then there is a unique natural transformation
\alpha\colon C_{k}^{\bowtie}\to G
such that

\alpha_{(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})}(x_{k})=g,

and a unique natural transformation
\beta\colon C_{k}^{\Delta}\to G
such that

\beta_{(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})}(y_{k})=g.

The proof of
[
Rot88
, Lemma 9.10]
describes the natural transformations of Lemma
5.10
: for
[\gamma]\in C_{k}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
, Lemma
5.9
gives
h_{\gamma}^{\bowtie}\colon(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})\to(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
such that
h_{\gamma}^{\bowtie}(x_{k})=[\gamma]
, and then
\alpha_{(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})}([\gamma])\coloneqq G(h_{\gamma}^{\bowtie})(g)
. Similarly,
\beta_{(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})}([\lambda])\coloneqq G(h_{\lambda}^{\Delta})(g)
for
[\lambda]\in C_{k}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
.

5.4.
Proof of the main theorem

To prove Theorem
5.3
we construct a chain equivalence between
C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}
and
C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}
inductively using
[
Rot88
, Theorem 9.12]
.

Lemma 5.11
.

The identity map
C_{0}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\coloneqq\mathbb{Z}X\overset{\id}{\to}\mathbb{Z}X\eqqcolon C_{0}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
induces a natural isomorphism
\widetilde{\operatorname{id}}:H_{0}^{\bowtie}\cong H_{0}^{\Delta}
.

Proof.

Fix a matched pair
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
with objects
X
. Identifying
C_{0}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
with
C_{0}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
via the identity map on
\mathbb{Z}X
, it suffices to show that
\im(d^{\bowtie})=\im(d^{\Delta})
in
\mathbb{Z}X
. If
[d,c]\in C_{1}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
, then
d^{\bowtie}[d,c]=[s(c)]-[r(d)]=d^{\Delta}[c,s(c)]+d^{\Delta}[r(d),d]\in\im(d^{\Delta}).

If
[c,d]\in C_{1}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
, then

d^{\Delta}[c,d]=[s(d)]-[r(c)]=d^{\bowtie}[c\bowtie d]\in\im(d^{\bowtie}).

∎

Proposition 5.12
.

There exist natural chain maps
\alpha\colon C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}\to C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}
and
\beta\colon C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}\to C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}
such that
\alpha\circ\beta
is naturally chain-homotopic to
\id_{C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}}
and
\beta\circ\alpha
is naturally chain-homotopic to
\id_{C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}}
such that
\alpha
and
\beta
lift the natural isomorphism
H_{0}^{\bowtie}\cong H_{0}^{\Delta}
, in the sense that the diagram

(5.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{2}^{\bowtie}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1}^{\bowtie}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\bowtie}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{\bowtie}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{2}^{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1}^{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{0}^{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{\Delta}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{2}^{\bowtie}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1}^{\bowtie}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\bowtie}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{\bowtie}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{\bowtie}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}^{\bowtie}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}^{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{\bowtie}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}^{\bowtie}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\operatorname{id}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\operatorname{id}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{2}}

of natural transformations commutes. If
\alpha^{\prime}\colon C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}\to C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}
and
\beta^{\prime}\colon C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}\to C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}
are chain maps that lift the natural isomorphism
H_{0}^{\bowtie}\cong H_{0}^{\Delta}
, then they are naturally chain-homotopic to
\alpha
and
\beta
.

For
k\geq 0
, let
x_{k},y_{k}
be as in (
5.8
) and (
5.9
). If for each
k\geq 0
,
\alpha_{k}\colon C_{k}^{\Delta}\to C_{k}^{\bowtie}
is a natural transformation such that
d^{\Delta}\circ\alpha_{k}(x_{k})=\alpha_{k-1}\circ d^{\Delta}(x_{k})
, then
\alpha=(\alpha_{k})
is a natural chain equivalence from

C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}
to
C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}
.
Similarly, if for each
k\geq 0
,
\beta_{k}\colon C_{k}^{\bowtie}\to C_{k}^{\Delta}
is a natural transformations such that
d^{\bowtie}\circ\beta_{k}(y_{k})=\beta_{k-1}\circ d^{\bowtie}(y_{k})
, then
\beta=(\beta_{k})
is a natural chain equivalence
from
C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}
to
C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}
.

The result is standard and follows from
[
Rot88
, Theorem 9.12]
, but we include some details to describe the resulting isomorphisms in homology explicitly.

Proof.

The morphisms
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
are induced by the identity maps on objects.
We start by constructing
\alpha
.
Suppose that there exists maps
\alpha_{n}
, for
n<k
such that the right-most
n+1
squares of (
5.10
) commute. Consider the matched pair
(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})
and let
x_{k}\in C_{k}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})
be as in (
5.8
). Commutativity of (
5.10
) implies that
d^{\Delta}\alpha_{k-1}d^{\bowtie}=(d^{\Delta})^{2}\alpha_{k-2}=0
. Lemma
5.7
implies that
(C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k}),d^{\bowtie})
is acyclic, so there exists
\overline{x}_{k}\in C_{k}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})
such that
d^{\Delta}(\overline{x}_{k})=\alpha_{k-1}d^{\bowtie}(x_{k})
. So Lemma
5.10

yields a unique natural transformation
\alpha_{k}\colon C_{k}^{\bowtie}\to C_{k}^{\Delta}
such that
\alpha_{k}^{(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})}(x_{k})=\overline{x}_{k}
and
d^{\Delta}\alpha_{k}=\alpha_{k-1}d^{\bowtie}
.

A similar construction using
y_{k}
and Lemma
5.8
gives
\beta_{k}
. By
[
Rot88
, Theorem 9.12]

\alpha_{k}
and
\beta_{k}
induce natural chain equivalences
\alpha
and
\beta
and these are, up to natural chain homotopy, the unique chain equivalences lifting the isomorphism of Lemma
5.11

∎

Proof of Theorem
5.3
.

The result follows from Proposition
5.12
and Theorem
5.5
.
∎

5.5.
Explicit formulas for the natural isomorphisms between homology theories

Proposition
5.12
yields a natural isomorphism
C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}\cong C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}
, and its final statement says how to recognise chain maps
\alpha
,
\beta
that induce such an isomorphism.
We show that the map
\Pi
of (
5.2
) and
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Pi$}\coloneqq\nabla\circ\Psi
are such chain maps, and describe chain maps inducing the remaining arrows in (
5.6
). We first examine how
\Pi_{k}
behaves on the model matched pairs
(\mathcal{E}_{m},\mathcal{F}_{m})
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Fix
m,\,k\geq 0
, and consider
\Pi_{k}\colon C_{k}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{E}_{m},\mathcal{F}_{m})\to C_{k}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{E}_{m},\mathcal{F}_{m})
. Then

\Pi_{k}\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{k}\rangle=\langle(p_{0},q_{0}),\ldots,(p_{k},q_{k})\rangle.

In particular, the element
y_{k}\in C_{k}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})
from (
5.9
), satisfies

(5.11)

\Pi_{k}(y_{k})=[f_{0,k-1}e_{0,k-1},f_{1,k-2}e_{1,k-2},\ldots,f_{k-2,1}e_{k-2,1},f_{k-1,0}e_{k-1,0}].

Proof.

We begin by establishing (
5.11
). We proceed by induction on
k
. The case
k=0
is trivial. Suppose inductively that the analogue of (
5.11
) describes
\Pi_{k-1}(y_{k-1})
. Recall that

y_{k}=[e_{0,k},e_{1,k},\ldots,e_{k-1,k};f_{k,k-1},\ldots,f_{k,1},f_{k,0}],

and let

\lambda_{k-1}\coloneqq[e_{1,k},\ldots,e_{k-1,k};f_{k,k-1},\ldots,f_{k,1}]\in C_{k}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{E}_{2(k-1)},\mathcal{F}_{2(k-1)}).

By Lemma
5.9
there exists a morphism
h_{\lambda_{k-1}}^{\Delta}\colon(\mathcal{E}_{2(k-1)},\mathcal{F}_{2(k-1)})\to(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})
such that
\lambda_{k-1}=C_{k-1}^{\Delta}(h_{\lambda_{k-1}}^{\Delta})(y_{k-1})
.
The inductive hypothesis gives

\displaystyle(1_{\mathcal{E}_{2k}}*\Pi_{k-1}*1_{\mathcal{F}_{2k}})(y_{k})=[e_{0,k},f_{1,k-1},e_{1,k-1},f_{2,k-2},\ldots,e_{k-2,2},f_{k-1,1},e_{k-1,1},f_{k,0}],

and applying
\bowtie^{k}
yields (
5.11
).

For the second statement, let
\gamma=\langle p_{0},\ldots,p_{k};q_{0},\ldots,q_{k}\rangle
. Lemma
5.9
gives a morphism
h_{\gamma}^{\Delta}\colon(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})\to(\mathcal{E}_{m},\mathcal{F}_{m})
such that

C_{k}^{\Delta}(h_{\gamma}^{\Delta})(y_{k})=\gamma
. Naturality of
\Pi_{k}
implies that
\Pi_{k}(\gamma)=C_{k}^{\bowtie}(h_{\gamma}^{\Delta})\circ\Pi_{k}(y_{k})
. So the result follows from (
5.11
) and the definition of
h_{\gamma}^{\Delta}
.
∎

Proposition 5.14
.

For
k\geq 0
, we have
\Pi_{k-1}\circ d^{\Delta}(y_{k})=d^{\bowtie}\circ\Pi_{k}(y_{k})
. In particular,
\Pi\colon C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}\to C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}
induces a natural isomorphism on homology.

Proof.

Fix
k\geq 0
. By Lemma
5.13
, for each
0\leq i\leq k
, we have

\displaystyle\Pi_{k-1}\circ\partial_{i}^{\Delta}(y_{k})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{k-1}\langle 0,1,\ldots,\widehat{i},\ldots,k-1,k;k,k-1,\ldots,\widehat{k-i},\ldots,1,0\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle(0,k),(1,k-1),\ldots,\widehat{(i,k-i)},\ldots,(k-1,1),(k,0)\rangle=\partial_{i}^{\bowtie}\circ\Pi_{k}(y_{k}).

A similar calculation gives
\Pi_{k+1}\circ\sigma_{i}^{\Delta}(y_{k})=\sigma_{i}^{\bowtie}\circ\Pi_{k}(y_{k})
and so
d^{\bowtie}\circ\Pi_{k}(y_{k})=\Pi_{k-1}\circ d^{\Delta}(y_{k})
. The final statement follows from
Proposition
5.12
.
∎

We next examine how the map
\Psi
of (
5.3
) behaves on the model matched pairs.

Lemma 5.15
.

Fix
k,\,m\geq 0
and
a=\langle\mathbf{a}_{0},\ldots\mathbf{a}_{k}\rangle\in C_{k}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{E}_{m},\mathcal{F}_{m})
. We have

(5.12)

\Psi_{k}\langle\mathbf{a}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{k}\rangle=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\langle a_{0}^{L},\ldots,a_{i}^{L};a_{i}^{R},\ldots,a_{k}^{R}\rangle.

In particular,
x_{k}\in C_{k}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathcal{F}_{2k})
as in (
5.8
), satisfies

(5.13)

\Psi_{k}(x_{k})=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\langle 0,1,\ldots,i-1,i;2k-i,2k-i-1,\ldots,k+1,k\rangle.

Proof.

The formula (
5.7
) gives a bijection between composable
q
-tuples in
\Gamma_{m}=\mathcal{E}_{m}\bowtie\mathcal{F}_{m}
and the set
X_{m}^{(q)}
. We claim that under this identification, if
(\mathbf{a}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{q})\in X_{m}^{(q)}
, then

(5.14)

\tau_{q}(\mathbf{a}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{q})=((a_{0}^{L},a_{0}^{R}),\ldots,(a_{0}^{L},a_{q-1}^{R}),(a_{0}^{L},a_{q}^{R}),(a_{1}^{L},a_{q}^{R})\ldots,(a_{q}^{L},a_{q}^{R}))

in
\Gamma_{m}^{q}
.
The tuples
((a_{0}^{L},a_{0}^{R}),(a_{0}^{L},a_{1}^{R}),\ldots,(a_{0}^{L},a_{q}^{R}))
and
((a_{0}^{L},a_{q}^{R}),\ldots,(a_{q-1}^{L},a_{q}^{R}),(a_{q}^{L},a_{q}^{R}))
belong to
\mathcal{E}_{m}^{q}\subseteq\Gamma_{m}^{q}
and
\mathcal{F}_{m}^{q}\subseteq\Gamma_{m}^{q}
. Since
r\times s\colon\Gamma_{m}\to X_{m}\times X_{m}
is injective, and factorisation in
\mathcal{E}_{m}^{*}\bowtie\mathcal{F}_{m}^{*}
is unique by
Proposition
3.20
, the formula (
5.14
) follows.

Since
\Psi_{p,q}=\rho_{p,q}\circ(\tau_{p}*\tau_{q})
by definition, and
\Psi_{k}=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\Psi_{i,k-i}
the identity (
5.12
) holds; and (
5.13
) follows from (
5.12
) since
x_{k}=\langle(0,2k),(1,2k-1),\ldots,(k,k)\rangle
.
∎

Proposition 5.16
.

For
k\geq 0
, we have
\Psi_{k-1}\circ d^{\bowtie}=d^{\Tot}\circ\Psi_{k}
. The chain maps
\Psi\colon C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}\to C_{\bullet}^{\Tot}
and
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Pi$}\colon C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}\to C_{\bullet}^{\Delta}
are natural chain equivalences inducing isomorphisms in homology.

Proof.

Let

a=\langle\mathbf{a}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{k}\rangle\in C_{k}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{E}_{m},\mathcal{F}_{m})
. For each
0\leq i\leq k
,

\displaystyle\Psi_{k-1}\circ\partial_{i}^{\bowtie}(a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq p<i}\langle a_{0}^{L},\ldots,a_{p}^{L};a_{p}^{R},\ldots,\widehat{a_{i}^{R}},\ldots,a_{k}^{R}\rangle+\sum_{i<p\leq k}\langle a_{0}^{L},\ldots,\widehat{a_{i}^{L}},\ldots,a_{p}^{L};a_{0}^{R},\ldots,a_{k}^{R}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq p<i}\partial^{v}_{i-p}\circ\Psi_{p,k-p}(a)+\sum_{i<p\leq k}\partial^{h}_{i}\circ\Psi_{p,k-p}(a).

Consequently,

\displaystyle\Psi_{k-1}\circ d^{\bowtie}=\sum_{0\leq p<i\leq k}(-1)^{i}\partial^{v}_{i-p}\circ\Psi_{p,k-p}+\sum_{0\leq i<p\leq k}(-1)^{i}\partial^{h}_{i}\circ\Psi_{p,k-p}.

Using at the third equality that,
\partial_{0}^{v}\circ\Psi_{0,k}=0
and
\partial_{k}^{h}\circ\Psi_{k,0}=0
, we calculate:

\displaystyle d^{\Tot}\circ\Psi_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{k}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{k-p}(-1)^{i+p}\partial_{i}^{v}\circ\Psi_{p,k-p}+\sum_{i=0}^{p}(-1)^{i}\partial_{i}^{h}\circ\Psi_{p,k-p}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq p<i\leq k}(-1)^{i}\partial_{i-p}^{v}\circ\Psi_{p,k-p}+\sum_{0\leq i<p\leq k}(-1)^{i}\partial_{i}^{h}\circ\Psi_{p,k-p}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{p=0}^{k}(-1)^{p}\partial_{0}^{v}\circ\Psi_{p,k-p}+(-1)^{p}\partial_{p}^{h}\circ\Psi_{p,k-p}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{k-1}\circ d^{\bowtie}+\sum_{t=0}^{k-1}(-1)^{t}(\partial_{0}^{v}\circ\Psi_{t,k-t}-\partial_{t+1}^{h}\circ\Psi_{t+1,k-t-1}).

So
d^{\Tot}\circ\Psi_{k}=\Psi_{k-1}\circ d^{\bowtie}
because

\displaystyle\partial_{0}^{v}\langle a_{0}^{L},\ldots,a_{t}^{L};a_{t}^{R},\ldots,a_{k}^{R}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle a_{0}^{L},\ldots,a_{t}^{L};a_{t+1}^{R},\ldots,a_{k}^{R}\rangle=\partial_{t+1}^{h}\langle a_{0}^{L},\ldots,a_{t+1}^{L};a_{t+1}^{R},\ldots,a_{k}^{R}\rangle.

Since
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Pi$}=\nabla\circ\Psi
and
\nabla
is a chain map,
d^{\Delta}_{k-1}\circ\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Pi$}_{k}(x_{k})=\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Pi$}_{k-1}\circ d^{\bowtie}_{k}(x_{k})
. So Proposition
5.12
shows that

\Pi

is a natural chain equivalence inverse to
\Pi
. Theorem
5.5
implies that
\Psi
is also a natural chain equivalence.
∎

To determine an explicit formula for
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Pi$}_{k}
we combine the formula (
5.3
) for
\Psi_{k}
with the formula (
5.1
) for the Eilenberg–Zilber map
\nabla_{k}
. Explicit formulae for the first few
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Pi$}_{i}
are

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Pi$}_{1}[d_{1}c_{1}]

\displaystyle=[c_{1};\_]+[\_;d_{1}]

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Pi$}_{2}[d_{1}c_{1},d_{2}c_{2}]

\displaystyle=[c_{1}\triangleleft d_{2},c_{2};\_,\_]+[c_{1},\_;\_,d_{2}]-[\_,c_{1};d_{2},\_]

\displaystyle\qquad+[\_,\_;d_{1},c_{1}\triangleright d_{2}]

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Pi$}_{3}[d_{1}c_{1},d_{2}c_{2},d_{3}c_{3}]

\displaystyle=[d_{1},c_{1}\triangleright d_{2},((c_{1}\triangleleft d_{2})c_{2})\triangleright d_{3};\_,\_,\_]+[\_,\_,c_{1};d_{2},c_{2}\triangleright d_{3},\_]

\displaystyle\qquad-[\_,c_{1},\_;d_{2},\_,c_{2}\triangleright d_{3}]+[c_{1},\_,\_;\_,d_{2},c_{2}\triangleright d_{3}]

\displaystyle\qquad+[\_,c_{1}\triangleleft d_{2},c_{2};d_{3},\_,\_]-[c_{1}\triangleleft d_{2},\_,c_{2};\_,d_{3},\_]

\displaystyle\qquad+[c_{1}\triangleleft d_{2},c_{2},\_;\_,\_,d_{3}]+[\_,\_,\_;c_{1}\triangleleft(d_{2}(c_{2}\triangleright d_{3})),c_{2}\triangleleft d_{3},c_{3}].

Remark 5.17
.

The formula (
5.3
) for
\Psi_{k}
was not initially obvious to us. We found formulae for
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Pi$}_{k}
for
k\leq 3
using a computer-aided search predicated on formulae that involved factorisation in
\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D}
of the element
d_{1}c_{1}\cdots d_{k}c_{k}
, interspersed with objects to obtain elements of
\mathcal{C}^{k}*\mathcal{D}^{k}
. We searched for, and found, integer coefficients that solved a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear equation ensuring a chain map that inverts
\Pi_{k}
on homology.
With those in hand, we could guess, and then check, a general formula for
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Pi$}_{k}
, and then reverse-engineer a formula for
\Psi_{k}
.

We can also translate between categorical and total chains using the maps
\Psi
and
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Psi$}=\Pi\circ\nabla
. For low-degree terms
\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Psi$}\colon\oplus_{p+q=k}\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{C}^{p}*\mathcal{D}^{q})\to C_{k}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
is given explicitly by

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Psi$}_{1}([c],[d])

\displaystyle=[c]+[d]

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Psi$}_{2}([c_{1},c_{2}],[c_{3};d_{1}],[d_{2},d_{3}])

\displaystyle=[c_{1},c_{2}]+[c_{3},d_{1}]-[c_{3}\triangleright d_{1},c_{3}\triangleleft d_{1}]+[d_{2},d_{3}]

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\Psi$}_{3}([c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}],[c_{4},c_{5};d_{1}],[c_{6};d_{2},d_{3}],[d_{4},d_{5},d_{6}])

\displaystyle=[c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}]+[(c_{4}c_{5})\triangleright d_{1},c_{4}\triangleleft(c_{5}\triangleright d_{1}),c_{5}\triangleright d_{1}]

\displaystyle\,-[c_{4},c_{5}\triangleright d_{1},c_{5}\triangleleft d_{1}]+[c_{4},c_{3},d_{1}]

\displaystyle\,+[c_{6},d_{2},d_{3}]-[c_{6}\triangleright d_{2},c_{6}\triangleleft d_{2},d_{3}]

\displaystyle\,+[c_{6}\triangleleft d_{2},(c_{6}\triangleleft d_{2})\triangleright d_{3},c_{6}\triangleleft(d_{2}d_{3})]+[d_{4},d_{5},d_{6}].

5.6.
A spectral sequence and a Künneth Theorem

5.6.1.
A spectral sequence

There is a spectral sequence that computes the total homology of a double complex; this and Theorem
5.3
compute of the homology of
\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D}
.

For fixed
p\in\mathbb{N}
, the sequence

(5.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{p,2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{p,1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{p,0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{v}_{p,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{v}_{p,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{v}_{p,0}}

(the
p
-th column of the double complex (
4.2
)) is a chain complex with homology groups

(5.16)

H^{v}_{p,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\coloneqq H_{q}(C_{p,\bullet},d^{v}_{p,\bullet}).

Since
d^{v}_{p,q}\circ d^{h}_{p,q+1}=-d^{h}_{p,q}\circ d^{v}_{p+1,q}
, the maps
d^{h}_{p,q}
descend to homomorphisms
\widetilde{d}^{h}_{p,q}\colon H^{v}_{p+1,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\to H^{v}_{p,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
. For each
q\in\mathbb{N}
, the sequence

(5.17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{v}_{2,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{v}_{1,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{v}_{0,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{d}^{h}_{2,q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{d}^{h}_{1,q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{d}^{h}_{0,q}}

is a chain complex. We define

H^{h}_{p}H^{v}_{q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
to be the
p
-th homology group of this complex,

H^{h}_{p}H^{v}_{q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\coloneqq H_{p}(H^{v}_{\bullet,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}),\widetilde{d}^{h}_{\bullet,q}).

We define
H^{v}_{q}H^{h}_{p}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
symmetrically by first considering rows of (
4.2
) and then columns:

H^{v}_{q}H^{h}_{p}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\coloneqq H_{q}(H^{h}_{p,\bullet}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}),\widetilde{d}^{v}_{p,\bullet}).

Corollary 5.18

(cf.
[
Wei94
, §5.6]
)
.

Let
C_{\bullet,\bullet}
be the matched complex of a matched pair
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
. Then there are homology spectral sequences
\{E_{p,q}^{hv,r},d^{hv,r}_{p,q}\}
and
\{E_{p,q}^{vh,r},d^{vh,r}_{p,q}\}
with first pages
E_{p,q}^{hv,1}=H^{v}_{p,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
and
E_{p,q}^{vh,1}=H^{h}_{p,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
with
d^{hv,1}=\widetilde{d}^{h}
and
d^{vh,1}=\widetilde{d}^{v}
, and second pages

E_{p,q}^{hv,2}=H^{h}_{p}H^{v}_{q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\quad\text{and}\quad E_{p,q}^{vh,2}=H^{v}_{q}H^{h}_{p}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}),

that both converge to the categorical homology of
\mathcal{C}\bowtie\mathcal{D}
.

We will use these spectral sequences to compute the homology of examples in Section
6
.

5.6.2.
The Künneth Theorem for products of monoids

Let
S
and
R
be monoids. Define a matched pair
(S,R)
by
s\triangleright r=r
and
s\triangleleft r=s
. The monoid
S\bowtie R
is just
S\times R
. There is an isomorphism of double complexes
C_{\bullet}(S)\ox_{\mathbb{Z}}C_{\bullet}(R)\cong C_{\bullet,\bullet}
taking
[s_{1},\ldots,s_{p}]\ox[r_{1},\ldots,r_{q}]
to
[s_{1},\ldots,s_{p},r_{1},\ldots,r_{q}]
.

Theorem
5.3
implies that
H_{\bullet}(S\times R)\cong H^{\Tot}_{\bullet}(S,R)=H^{\Tot}(C_{\bullet,\bullet})
. So we recover the Künneth formula
[
Wei94
, Theorem 3.6.3]
: an unnaturally split exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{p+q=n}H_{p}(S)\ox H_{q}(R)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}(S\times R)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{p+q=n-1}\Tor^{\mathbb{Z}}_{1}(H_{p}(S),H_{q}(R))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

6.
Examples and homology computations

In this section (specifically in Section
6.4
) we use our results to compute the homology of matched pairs that are like pullbacks of odometer actions over the path categories of directed graphs. The technical results we develop along the way apply to more-general systems such as Exel–Pardo self-similar systems and
k
-graphs.

We first consider, in Section
6.1
, matched pairs
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
in which
\mathcal{D}=E^{*}
for a directed graph
E
. We show that the vertical homology
H^{v}_{p,\bullet}(\mathcal{C},E^{*})
of (
5.16
) vanishes above degree
1
.
This is unsurprising since directed graphs are
1
-dimensional; but we could not find a general theorem that applies, so we prove that
H^{v}_{p,q}(\mathcal{C},E^{*})=0
for
q\geq 2
by direct computation.

In Section
6.2
we consider matched pairs
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
where
\mathcal{C}
is a disjoint union

\bigsqcup_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathcal{C}_{u}
of monoids. We describe an isomorphism between
H^{h}_{\bullet,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
and the direct sum

\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}_{u};\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})
of the homology of the monoids
\mathcal{C}_{v}
with
coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}
. The Universal Coefficient Theorem gives a short exact sequence that computes
H^{h}_{\bullet,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})

as an extension of an appropriate
\Tor
-group by
\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}H_{p}(\mathcal{C}_{u})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{C}_{u}}\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}
.

In Section
6.3
we restrict further to
\mathcal{C}_{u}\cong\mathbb{Z}
. We deduce that
H^{h}_{\bullet,q}(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{D}^{0},\mathcal{D})
vanishes in degree 2 or more, and compute
H^{h}_{0,q}(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{D}^{0},\mathcal{D})
and
H^{h}_{1,q}(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{D}^{0},\mathcal{D})
in terms of the groups of invariants and coinvariants
\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}
.

Finally, in Section
6.4
we compute
H^{\bowtie}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{Z}\times F^{0},F^{*})
for matched pairs consisting of a bundle of copies of
\mathbb{Z}
acting like odometers on the path category of a directed graph
F
. We show that in the second spectral sequence of Corollary
5.18
, only
E^{vh,2}_{0,0}
,
E^{vh,2}_{1,0}
,
E^{vh,2}_{0,1}
, and
E^{vh,2}_{1,1}
can be nonzero. So the sequence converges on its second page, yielding an explicit formula for
H_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}(\mathbb{Z}\times F^{0},F^{*})
in terms of a weighted incidence matrix in
M_{F^{0},F^{1}}(\mathbb{Z})
(Proposition
6.14
).

6.1.
Matched pairs involving path categories of directed graphs

Let
E
be a directed graph and suppose that
(\mathcal{C},E^{*})
is a matched pair. We say that
(\mathcal{C},E^{*})
is a
length-preserving
matched pair if
|c\triangleright\alpha|=|\alpha|
for all
(c,\alpha)\in\mathcal{C}*E^{*}
.

We write
N_{p,q}
for the subgroup of
C_{p,q}
generated
by the nondegenerate vertical chains: chains
[c;d_{1},\ldots,d_{q}]
such that

d_{i}\notin\mathcal{D}^{0}
for all
1\leq i\leq q
. By

[
Wei94
, Theorem 8.3.8]
, the group
H_{p,q}^{v}(\mathcal{C},E^{*})
of (
5.16
) is isomorphic to the
q
-th homology group of
(N_{p,\bullet},d^{v}_{p,\bullet}|_{N_{p,\bullet}})
.

For
\alpha\in E^{*}
we write
\alpha^{i}
for the
i
-th edge of

\alpha
. So
\alpha=\alpha^{1}\alpha^{2}\cdots\alpha^{|\alpha|}
. For
0\leq i\leq j\leq|\alpha|
, we define
\alpha^{[i,j]}\in E^{j-i}
by
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{[i,j]}\alpha^{\prime\prime}
for some
\alpha^{\prime}\in E^{i}
and

\alpha^{\prime\prime}\in E^{|\alpha|-j}
. For example,
\alpha^{[i-1,i]}=\alpha^{i}
for
1\leq i\leq|\alpha|
, and
\alpha=\alpha^{[0,i-1]}\alpha^{i}\alpha^{[i,|\alpha|]}
for each

i
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small category and let
E
be a directed graph, and suppose that
(\mathcal{C},E^{*})
is a length-preserving matched pair. Taking the convention that the empty sum is zero, for
p\geq 0
and
q\geq 1
, define
s^{v}_{p,q}\colon N_{p,q}\to N_{p,q+1}
by

s^{v}_{p,q}[c;\alpha]=-\sum_{i=1}^{|\alpha_{1}|-1}[c\triangleleft\alpha^{[0,i-1]}_{1};\alpha^{i}_{1},\alpha^{[i,|\alpha_{1}|]}_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{q}]

for
c\in\mathcal{C}^{p}
and
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{q})\in(E^{*})^{q}
with
s(c)=r(\alpha_{1})
. Then for
q\geq 2
,

(6.1)

d^{v}_{p,q}s^{v}_{p,q}+s^{v}_{p,q-1}d^{v}_{p,q-1}=\id_{N_{p,q}}.

In particular, for
q\geq 2
we have
H^{v}_{p,q}(\mathcal{C},E^{*})=0
, and there is a long exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{p+1}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{C},E^{*})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{hv,2}_{p+1,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{p-1,1}^{hv,2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{p}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{C},E^{*})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{p,0}^{hv,2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{p-2,1}^{hv,2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{p-1}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{C},E^{*})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

Proof.

Fix
q\geq 2
. We write
d\coloneqq\bigoplus_{p}d^{v}_{p,q}
,
s=\bigoplus_{p}s^{v}_{p,q}
, and
N_{q}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{p}N_{p,q}
. The restrictions
\partial_{j}\colon N_{q}\to N_{q-1}
of the face maps satisfy

\partial_{j}([c;\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}])=\begin{cases}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1};\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}]&\text{ if $j=0$}\\
[c;\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{j}\alpha_{j+1},\dots,\alpha_{q}]&\text{ if $1\leq j\leq q-1$}\\
[c;\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q-1}]&\text{ if $j=q$},\end{cases}

and
d_{q}=\sum^{q}_{j=0}(-1)^{j}\partial_{j}
.

We first claim that for
j\geq 3
we have
\partial_{j}\circ s=s\circ\partial_{j-1}
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\partial_{j}(s([c;\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}]))

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{|\alpha_{1}|-1}\partial_{j}([c\triangleleft\alpha^{[0,i-1]}_{1};\alpha^{i}_{1},\alpha^{[i,|\alpha_{1}|]}_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{q}])

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{|\alpha_{1}|-1}[c\triangleleft\alpha^{[0,i-1]}_{1};\alpha^{i}_{1},\alpha^{[i,|\alpha_{1}|]}_{1},\partial_{j-2}(\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{q})]

\displaystyle=s([c;\alpha_{1},\partial_{j-2}(\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q})])

\displaystyle=s(\partial_{j-1}([c;\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}])).

Using this at the final equality, we obtain

\displaystyle d\circ s+s\circ d

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{q+1}(-1)^{i}\partial_{i}\circ s+\sum_{j=0}^{q}(-1)^{j}s\circ\partial_{j}

\displaystyle=\partial_{0}\circ s-\partial_{1}\circ s+\partial_{2}\circ s+\Big(\sum^{q+1}_{i=3}(-1)^{i}\partial_{i}\circ s\Big)+s\circ\partial_{0}-s\circ\partial_{1}+\Big(\sum_{j=2}^{q}(-1)^{j}s\circ\partial_{j}\Big)

\displaystyle=\partial_{0}\circ s-\partial_{1}\circ s+\partial_{2}\circ s+s\circ\partial_{0}-s\circ\partial_{1}+\sum^{q+1}_{j=3}\Big((-1)^{j}\partial_{j}\circ s-(-1)^{j}s\circ\partial_{j-1}\Big)

\displaystyle=\partial_{0}\circ s-\partial_{1}\circ s+\partial_{2}\circ s+s\circ\partial_{0}-s\circ\partial_{1}.

So it suffices to show that

(6.2)

\partial_{0}\circ s-\partial_{1}\circ s+\partial_{2}\circ s+s\circ\partial_{0}-s\circ\partial_{1}=\id_{N_{q}}.

For this, fix

x\coloneqq[c;\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}]\in N_{q}
. Let
l\coloneqq|\alpha_{1}|
. We claim that

(6.3)

(\partial_{0}\circ s-\partial_{1}\circ s)(x)=x-[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,l-1]};\alpha_{1}^{l},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}].

To see this, we compute:

\displaystyle\partial_{0}\circ s(x)

\displaystyle=\partial_{0}\big(-\sum^{l-1}_{i=1}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i-1]};\alpha_{1}^{i},\alpha_{1}^{[i,l]},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}]\Big)=-\sum^{l-1}_{i=1}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i]};\alpha_{1}^{[i,l]},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}],\text{ and}

\displaystyle\partial_{1}\circ s(x)

\displaystyle=\partial_{1}\Big(-\sum^{l-1}_{i=1}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i-1]};\alpha_{1}^{i},\alpha_{1}^{[i,l]},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}]\Big)=-\sum^{l-1}_{i=1}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i-1]};\alpha_{1}^{[i-1,l]},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}].

So

(\partial_{0}\circ s-\partial_{1}\circ s)(x)=\sum^{l-1}_{i=1}\big(-[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i]};\alpha_{1}^{[i,l]},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}]+[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i-1]};\alpha_{1}^{[i-1,l]},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}]\big)

telescopes to (
6.3
).
Next, we claim that

(6.4)

(s\circ\partial_{0}-s\circ\partial_{1})(x)=\Big(\sum^{l-1}_{i=1}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i-1]};\alpha_{1}^{i};\alpha_{1}^{[i,l]}\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\dots,\alpha_{q}]\Big)+[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,l-1]};\alpha_{1}^{l},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\dots,\alpha_{q}].

Let
m\coloneqq|\alpha_{2}|
. Again, we compute:

(6.5)

s\circ\partial_{0}(x)=s([c\triangleleft\alpha_{1};\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}])=-\sum^{m-1}_{i=1}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{2}^{[0,i-1]};\alpha_{2}^{i},\alpha_{2}^{[i,m]},\alpha_{3},\dots,\alpha_{q}],

and

\displaystyle s\circ\partial_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=s([c;\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}])

\displaystyle=-\sum^{l+m-1}_{i=1}[c\triangleleft(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2})^{[0,i-1]};(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2})^{i},(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2})^{[i,l+m]},\alpha_{3},\dots,\alpha_{q}]

\displaystyle=-\Big(\sum^{l-1}_{i=1}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i-1]};\alpha_{1}^{i},\alpha_{1}^{[i,l]}\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\dots,\alpha_{q}]\Big)-[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,l-1]};\alpha_{1}^{l},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}]

\displaystyle\qquad-\Big(\sum^{m-1}_{i=1}[c\triangleleft(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}^{[0,i-1]});\alpha_{2}^{i},\alpha_{2}^{[i,m]},\alpha_{3},\dots,\alpha_{q}]\Big).

Subtracting (
6.5
) from this equation yields (
6.4
).
Now we add Equations (
6.4
) and (
6.3
), and the terms
[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,l-1]};\alpha_{1}^{l},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\dots,\alpha_{q}]
cancel, giving

(6.6)

(\partial_{0}\circ s-\partial_{1}\circ s+s\circ\partial_{0}-s\circ\partial_{1})(x)=x+\Big(\sum^{l-1}_{i=1}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i-1]};\alpha_{1}^{i},\alpha_{1}^{[i,l]}\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\dots,\alpha_{q}]\Big).

Finally, we compute

\partial_{2}\circ s(x)=\partial_{2}\Big(-\sum^{l-1}_{i=1}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i-1]};\alpha_{1}^{i},\alpha_{1}^{[i,l]},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}]\Big)=-\sum^{l-1}_{i=1}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i-1]};\alpha_{1}^{i},\alpha_{1}^{[i,l]}\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}].

Adding this to (
6.6
) gives

(\partial_{0}\circ s-\partial_{1}\circ s+\partial_{2}\circ s+s\circ\partial_{0}-s\circ\partial_{1})(x)=x,

which gives (
6.2
).

Now fix
a\in\ker(d_{q-1})\cap N_{q}
. Then (
6.1
) gives

a=d_{q}(s_{q}(a))+s_{q-1}(d_{q-1}(a))=d_{q}(s_{q}(a))\in\ran(d_{q}),

so
H_{q}(N_{p,\bullet})=0
. Theorem 8.3.8 of
[
Wei94
]
gives
H_{p,q}^{v}(\mathcal{C},E^{*})=H_{q}(N_{p,\bullet})=0
.
The long exact sequence exists by definition of convergence of a spectral sequence
[
Wei94
, p.124]
.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

The proof of the preceding lemma relies on treating the empty sum as zero, prompting a quick reality check of the edge-case where
[c;\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}]\in N_{q}
with
\alpha_{1}=e\in E^{1}
.
Let
x=[c;e,\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}]
. Since
s^{v}_{p,q}(x)=0
, (
6.1
) for
x
collapses to
s^{v}_{p,q-1}\circ d^{v}_{p,q-1}(x)=x
, so the cancellation that led to (
6.2
) appears to fall down.

But all is well: for
j\geq 3
we have
\partial_{j}([c;e,\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{q}])=[c;e,\kappa]
for some
\kappa\in(E^{*})^{q-1}
, and so
s^{v}_{p,q-1}(\partial_{j}(x))=s^{v}_{p,q-1}([c;e,\kappa])=0
. So
s^{v}_{p,q-1}\circ d^{v}_{p,q-1}(x)=s^{v}_{p,q-1}([c\triangleleft e;\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}])-s^{v}_{p,q-1}([c;e\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}])
and all the terms in the resulting sums cancel except the first term
-(-[c;e,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}])=x
of
s^{v}_{p,q-1}([c;e\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}])
.

Recall that
\{E_{p,q}^{vh,r},d_{p,q}^{vh,r}\}
is the spectral sequence of Corollary
5.18
, which in our current setup satisfies
E_{p,q}^{vh,2}=H^{v}_{p}H^{h}_{q}(\mathcal{C},E^{*})
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small category and let
E
be a directed graph, and suppose that
(\mathcal{C},E^{*})
is a length-preserving matched pair.
For
p\geq 0
and
q\geq 2
, we have

s^{v}_{p,q}\circ d^{h}_{p,q}=d^{h}_{p,q+1}\circ s^{v}_{p+1,q}
. We have
E^{vh,2}_{p,q}=0
for all
q\geq 2
,
H_{0}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{C},E^{*})\cong E^{vh,2}_{0,0}
, and for each
n\geq 1
there is a short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{vh,2}_{0,n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{n}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{C},E^{*})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{vh,2}_{1,n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Proof.

Fix
(c;\alpha)\in\mathcal{C}^{p+1}*({E^{*}})^{q}
. We compute

\displaystyle d^{h}_{p,q+1}\circ s^{v}_{p+1,q}[c;\alpha]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{|\alpha_{1}|-1}\sum_{k=0}^{p+1}(-1)^{k}\partial^{h,k}_{p,q+1}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i-1]};\alpha_{1}^{i},\alpha_{1}^{[i,|\alpha_{1}|]},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{q}].

Analogously to Lemma
4.4
, we observe that for
0\leq k\leq p
,

\displaystyle\partial^{h,k}_{p,q+1}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i-1]};\alpha_{1}^{i},\alpha_{1}^{[i,|\alpha_{1}|]},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{q}]

\displaystyle=[\partial_{p,0}^{h,k}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i-1]}];\alpha_{1}^{i},\alpha_{1}^{[i,|\alpha_{1}|]},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{q}]

\displaystyle=[\partial_{p,0}^{h,k}[c]\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i-1]};\alpha_{1}^{i},\alpha_{1}^{[i,|\alpha_{1}|]},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{q}].

Write
(c_{p}\triangleright\alpha)_{r}
for the
r
-th entry of
c\triangleright\alpha\in(E^{*})^{q}
. For
k=p+1
we have

\displaystyle\partial^{h,p+1}_{p,q+1}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[1,i-1]};\alpha_{1}^{i},\alpha_{1}^{[i,|\alpha_{1}|]},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{q}]

\displaystyle\quad=[\partial_{p,0}^{h,p+1}[c\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i-1]}];(c_{p}\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[0,i-1]})\triangleright(\alpha_{1}^{i},\alpha_{1}^{[i,|\alpha_{1}|]},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{q})]

\displaystyle\quad=[\partial_{p,0}^{h,p+1}[c]\triangleleft\alpha_{1}^{[1,i-1]};(c_{p}\triangleright\alpha)_{1}^{i},(c_{p}\triangleright\alpha)_{1}^{[i,|\alpha_{1}|]},(c_{p}\triangleright\alpha)_{2},\ldots,(c_{p}\triangleright\alpha)_{q}].

Hence,

\displaystyle d^{h}_{p,q+1}\circ s^{v}_{p+1,q}[c;\alpha]=\sum_{k=0}^{p+1}(-1)^{k}s^{v}_{p,q}\circ\partial_{p,q}^{h,k}[c;\alpha]=s^{v}_{p,q}\circ d^{h}_{p,q}[c;\alpha].

Thus, the
s^{v}_{p,q}
descend to sections of the differentials between the
E^{vh,1}_{p,q}
, and the resulting homology groups
E^{vh,2}_{p,q}
vanish for
q\geq 2
. So the spectral sequence stabilises by the second page, and short exact sequences follow from the associated filtration.
∎

6.2.
Matched pairs involving bundles of monoids

In this section
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
is a matched pair in which
\mathcal{C}=\bigsqcup_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathcal{C}_{u}
is a bundle of monoids over
\mathcal{C}^{0}=\mathcal{D}^{0}
.
For each
u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}
and
q\geq 0
, the free
\mathbb{Z}
-module
\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}
generated by
u\mathcal{D}^{q}=\{d\in\mathcal{D}^{q}\mid r(d)=u\}
is a left
\mathcal{C}_{u}
-module under the action
c\cdot\big(\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{q}}n_{d}d\big)=\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{q}}n_{d}(c\triangleright d)
.

For a monoid
S
, the categories of left (respectively right)
S
-modules and left (respectively right)
\mathbb{Z}[S]
-modules are equivalent. Given a left
S
-module
M
we can compute the homology
H_{\bullet}(S;M)
of
S
with coefficients in
M
as follows: fix a projective resolution
\cdots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}\to 0
of the trivial
S
-module
\mathbb{Z}
. Then
H_{\bullet}(S;M)
is the homology of the chain complex
\cdots\to P_{1}\ox_{\mathbb{Z}[S]}M\to P_{0}\ox_{\mathbb{Z}[S]}M\to 0
: that is,
H_{n}(S;M)\coloneqq\Tor_{n}^{\mathbb{Z}[S]}(\mathbb{Z};M)
.

By taking the
bar resolution
of the trivial (right)
S
-module
\mathbb{Z}
we arrive at a more familiar description. For
n\geq 1
let
B_{n}
be the free
S
-module generated by
\{[s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}]\mid s_{i}\in S\}
. Let
B_{0}
be the free
S
-module generated by the symbol
[\phantom{\cdot}]
. Define
b_{n}\colon B_{n+1}\to B_{n}
by

(6.7)

b_{n}[s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}]=[s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}]+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}[s_{0},\ldots,s_{i-1}s_{i},\ldots,s_{n}]+(-1)^{n+1}[s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1}]s_{n}

for
n\geq 1
and
b_{-1}\colon B_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}
by
b_{-1}[\phantom{\cdot}]=1
; note that
b_{0}[s_{0}]=[\phantom{\cdot}]-[\phantom{\cdot}]s_{0}
. The group of
p
-chains with values in
M
is

C_{p}(S;M)\coloneqq B_{p}\ox_{\mathbb{Z}[S]}M.

The boundary maps
d_{p}\coloneqq b_{p}\ox\id_{M}\colon C_{p+1}(S;M)\to C_{p}(S;M)
for
p\geq 1
satisfy

\displaystyle d_{p}([s_{0},\ldots,s_{p}]\ox m)

\displaystyle=[s_{1},\ldots,s_{p}]\ox m+\sum_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{i}[s_{0},\ldots,s_{i-1}s_{i},\ldots,s_{p}]\ox m

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p+1}[s_{0},\ldots,s_{p-1}]\ox s_{p}\cdot m,

and
d_{0}\colon C_{1}(S;M)\to C_{0}(S;M)
satisfies
d_{0}([s_{0}]\ox m)=[\phantom{\cdot}]\ox m-[\phantom{\cdot}]\ox s_{0}\cdot m
.
Then
H_{n}(S;M)\cong\ker(d_{n-1})/\im(d_{n})
. Taking
M=\mathbb{Z}
, the trivial
S
-module, recovers Definition
4.1
if
\mathcal{C}=S
.

Given a matched pair
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
where
\mathcal{C}
is a bundle of monoids, we can compute the horizontal homology of the matched complex using the homology of the monoids
\mathcal{C}_{u}
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a small category and let
\mathcal{C}=\bigsqcup_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathcal{C}_{u}
be a bundle of monoids over
\mathcal{D}^{0}
such that
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
is a matched pair. Then
C_{p}(\mathcal{C}_{u};\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})\ni[c_{0},\ldots,c_{p}]\ox d\mapsto[c_{0},\ldots,c_{p-1};c_{p}\triangleright d]\in C_{p,q}
induces an isomorphism

H^{h}_{p,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\cong\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}H_{p}(\mathcal{C}_{u};\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}),

and there is a short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}H_{p}(\mathcal{C}_{u})\ox_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{C}_{u}]}\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H^{h}_{p,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\Tor^{\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{C}_{u}]}_{1}(H_{p-1}(\mathcal{C}_{u}),\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Proof.

In
C_{p}(\mathcal{C}_{u};\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})=B_{p}\ox_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{C}_{u}]}\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}
we have
[c_{1},\ldots,c_{p}]\ox d=[c_{1},\ldots,c_{p-1},s(c_{p-1})]\ox c_{p}\cdot d
.
Consequently, the map
\pi_{p,q}\colon\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}C_{p}(\mathcal{C}_{u};\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})\to C_{p,q}
defined by

\pi_{p,q}([c_{0},\ldots,c_{p}]\ox d)=[c_{0},\ldots,c_{p-1};c_{p}\triangleright d]

for
[c_{0},\ldots,c_{p}]\ox d\in C_{p}(\mathcal{C}_{u};\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})
and
u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}
, is an isomorphism. Moreover,

\displaystyle d^{h}_{p,q}\circ\pi_{p+1,q}([c_{0},\ldots,c_{p+1}]\ox d)

\displaystyle=d^{h}_{p,q}[c_{1},\ldots,c_{p},c_{p+1}\triangleright d]

\displaystyle=[c_{1},\ldots,c_{p};c_{p+1}\triangleright d]+\sum_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{i}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{i-1}c_{i},\ldots,c_{p};c_{p+1}\triangleright d]

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{p+1}[c_{0},\ldots,c_{p-1},c_{p}c_{p+1}\triangleright d]

\displaystyle=\pi_{p,q}\circ d^{u}_{p,q}([c_{0},\ldots,c_{p+1}]\ox d).

So
H^{h}_{p,q}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\cong\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}H_{p}(\mathcal{C}_{u};\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})
.

Since
\mathbb{Z}
is a free
\mathcal{C}_{u}
-module the short exact sequence follows from
[
Wei94
, Theorem 3.6.1]
.
∎

6.3.
Matched pairs involving integer bundles

In this section we consider matched pairs
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
where
\mathcal{C}\cong\mathcal{D}^{0}\times\mathbb{Z}
. If
M
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-module, then
M^{\mathbb{Z}}=\{m\in M\mid k\cdot m=m\text{ for all }k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
is its submodule of
invariants
and
M_{\mathbb{Z}}=M/\langle k\cdot m-m\mid k\in\mathbb{Z},\,m\in M\rangle
is its module of
coinvariants
.

If
X
is a set and
\triangleright\colon\mathbb{Z}\times X\to X
is a left action, we write
\mathbb{Z}\backslash X
for the set of
\mathbb{Z}
-orbits in
X
. We denote the orbit of
x\in X
by
[\![x]\!]\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash X
, and the set of periodic points of
X
by
