\Per(X)\coloneqq\{x\in X\mid\text{there exists }k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\text{ such that }k\triangleright x=x\}.

Then
\mathbb{Z}\backslash{\Per(X)}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}\backslash X
is the set of finite orbits in
X
.

Each left action
\triangleright\colon\mathbb{Z}\times X\to X
induces a corresponding left action
\cdot\colon\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}X\to\mathbb{Z}X
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
X
be a set, and
\triangleright\colon\mathbb{Z}\times X\to X
a left action. There are isomorphisms

\phi_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}(\mathbb{Z}\backslash X)\to(\mathbb{Z}X)_{\mathbb{Z}}\quad\text{and}\quad\phi_{1}\colon\mathbb{Z}(\mathbb{Z}\backslash\!\Per(X))\to(\mathbb{Z}X)^{\mathbb{Z}}

such that
\phi_{0}([\![d]\!])=d+\langle k\cdot m-m\mid k\in\mathbb{Z},\,m\in\mathbb{Z}X\rangle
and
\phi_{1}([\![d]\!])=\sum_{d^{\prime}\in[\![d]\!]}d^{\prime}
.

Proof.

For the first isomorphism, regard
\mathbb{Z}X
and
\mathbb{Z}(\mathbb{Z}\backslash X)
as the sets of finitely supported
\mathbb{Z}
-valued functions on
X
and
\mathbb{Z}\backslash X
respectively. Define
\pi\colon\mathbb{Z}X\to\mathbb{Z}(\mathbb{Z}\backslash X)
by
\pi(f)([\![x]\!])=\sum_{y\in[\![x]\!]}f(y)
. Then
\ker(\pi)=\langle k\cdot m-m\mid k\in\mathbb{Z},\,m\in\mathbb{Z}X\rangle
, so
\pi
descends to an isomorphism
(\mathbb{Z}X)_{\mathbb{Z}}\cong\mathbb{Z}(\mathbb{Z}\backslash X)
whose inverse is the desired map
\phi_{0}
.

For the second isomorphism, fix
m\coloneqq\sum_{x\in X}a_{x}x\in\mathbb{Z}X
, where each
a_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
\sum_{x\in X}a_{x}x=\sum_{[\![y]\!]\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash X}\sum_{x\in[\![y]\!]}a_{x}x
. For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

k\cdot m-m=\sum_{[\![y]\!]\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash X}\Big(\sum_{x\in[\![y]\!]}a_{x}(k\triangleright x)-a_{x}x\Big).

Hence,
k\cdot m-m=0
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
if and only if
a_{x}=a_{y}
whenever
[\![x]\!]=[\![y]\!]
; that is,
a\colon x\mapsto a_{x}
is constant on orbits. Since
a
is finitely supported, if
m\in(\mathbb{Z}X)^{\mathbb{Z}}
then
a
is nonzero only on finite orbits. Hence, the formula for
\phi_{1}
determines an isomorphism.
∎

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
be a matched pair with
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{D}^{0}\times\mathbb{Z}
. There are isomorphisms
\alpha_{0}\colon\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\backslash u\mathcal{D}^{q})\to H_{0,q}^{h}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
and
\alpha_{1}\colon\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\backslash\Per(u\mathcal{D}^{q}))\to H_{1,q}^{h}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
satisfying

(6.8)

\alpha_{0}([\![d]\!])=[r(d);d]+\im(d^{h}_{0,q})\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha_{1}([\![d]\!])=\sum_{d^{\prime}\in[\![d]\!]}[1;d^{\prime}]+\im(d_{1,q}^{h}).

Moreover,

(6.9)

H_{p,q}^{h}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\cong\begin{cases}\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}(\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})_{\mathbb{Z}}&\text{if }p=0\\
\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}(\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})^{\mathbb{Z}}&\text{if }p=1\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\cong\begin{cases}\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\backslash u\mathcal{D}^{q})&\text{if }p=0\\
\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\backslash\Per(u\mathcal{D}^{q}))&\text{if }p=1\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

We identify the group ring
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]
with the ring of Laurent polynomials
\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}]
. Let
\Sigma\colon\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}]\to\mathbb{Z}
be evaluation at 1, the homomorphism that sums coefficients.

As in
[
Wei94
, Example 6.1.4]
,

(6.10)

\cdots\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}]\overset{\times(t-1)}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}]\overset{\Sigma}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow 0,

is a projective resolution of
\mathbb{Z}
by
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]
-modules.
Since any projective resolution computes the homology of a group, it follows that
H_{0}(\mathbb{Z};\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})\cong(\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})_{\mathbb{Z}}
and
H_{1}(\mathbb{Z};\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})\cong(\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, and that
H_{n}(\mathbb{Z};\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})=0
for
n\geq 2
. The first isomorphism of (
6.9
) follows from Proposition
6.4
; the second follows from Lemma
6.5
.

Let
N_{q}\coloneqq\langle k\cdot m-m\mid k\in\mathbb{Z},\,m\in\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}\rangle
.
To establish (
6.8
) we describe the chain map connecting the bar resolution (
6.7
) with the resolution (
6.10
). Let
\delta_{n}
be the generator of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]
corresponding to
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and let
[n]
be the basis element of the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]
-module
B_{1}
(by definition,
B_{1}
is the free
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]
-module over
\mathbb{Z}
, but who wants to write
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}][\mathbb{Z}]
?) Then the diagram

(6.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}[t,t^{-1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times(t-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{n}\mapsto[1]\cdot\delta_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{n}\mapsto[\phantom{\cdot}]\cdot\delta_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}

commutes. The homology
H_{\bullet}(\mathbb{Z};\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})
as computed by each of these resolutions is obtained by tensoring by
\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}
on the right, replacing
\Sigma\otimes 1
and
b_{-1}\otimes 1
with
0
, and taking homology.

Hence, for each
u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}
, the vertical map
t^{n}\mapsto[\phantom{\cdot}]\cdot\delta_{n}
in (
6.11
) induces an isomorphism
\coker(\times(t-1)\otimes\id_{\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}})\to H_{0}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})
taking
t^{0}\otimes d+\im(\times(t-1)\otimes\id_{\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}})
to
d+\im(b_{0})
. The isomorphism
(\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})_{\mathbb{Z}}\to\coker(\times(t-1)\otimes\id_{\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}})\to H_{0}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})
of
[
Wei94
, Example 6.1.4]
carries
d+N_{q}
to
t^{0}\otimes d+\im(\times(t-1)\otimes\id_{\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}})
. So composing these maps gives an isomorphism
\psi_{u,0}\colon(\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})_{\mathbb{Z}}\to H_{0}(\mathbb{Z};\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})
such that
\psi_{u,0}(d+N_{q})=d+\im(b_{0}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}})=d+\im(d^{h}_{0,q})
.

Similarly,
t^{n}\mapsto[1]\cdot\delta_{n}
restricts to an isomorphism
\ker(\times(t-1)\otimes\id_{\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}})\to H_{1}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})
taking
\sum_{d}k_{d}(t^{0}\otimes d)
to
\sum_{d}k_{d}[1;d]+\im(d_{1,q}^{h})
. The isomorphism
(\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})^{\mathbb{Z}}\to\ker(\times(t-1)\otimes\id_{\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q}})
of
[
Wei94
, Example 6.1.4]
carries
\sum k_{d}d
to
\sum k_{d}(t^{0}\otimes d)
. So composing these maps yields an isomorphism
\psi_{u,1}\colon(\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})^{\mathbb{Z}}\to H_{1}(\mathbb{Z};\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})
given by
\psi_{u,1}(\sum_{d}k_{d}d)=\sum_{d}k_{d}[1;d]+\im(d_{1,q}^{h})
.

Lemma
6.5
gives an isomorphism
\phi_{u,0}\colon\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\backslash u\mathcal{D}^{q})\to(\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})_{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\phi_{u,0}([\![d]\!])=d+N_{q}
and an isomorphism
\phi_{u,1}\colon\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\backslash\Per(u\mathcal{D}^{q}))\to(\mathbb{Z}u\mathcal{D}^{q})^{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\phi_{u,1}([\![d]\!])=\sum_{d^{\prime}\in[\![d]\!]}d^{\prime}
. The maps
\alpha_{i}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\psi_{u,i}\circ\phi_{u,i}
satisfy (
6.8
).
∎

The next lemma helps to compute the terms
E^{vh}_{p,q}
in Corollary
5.18
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
be a matched pair with
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{D}^{0}\times\mathbb{Z}
. For

d=(d_{0},\dots,d_{q})\in\Per(\mathcal{D}^{q+1})
, let
O(d)\coloneqq\min\{n\geq 1\mid n\triangleright d=d\}
. For each

0\leq i\leq q
, let
\partial_{i}\colon\mathcal{D}^{q+1}\to\mathcal{D}^{q}
be the face map
of (
4.1
). Regarding
O(d)\triangleleft d\in\{s(d)\}\times\mathbb{Z}
as
an integer, for
0\leq i\leq q
define

\rho_{i}(d)\coloneqq\begin{cases}(O(d)\triangleleft d_{0})/O(\partial_{0}(d))&\text{ if $i=0$}\\
O(d)/O(\partial_{i}(d))&\text{ if $i\geq 1$.}\end{cases}

Then
\rho_{i}(d)
is a nonnegative integer for each
i
.

Proof.

First suppose that
i\geq 1
. It suffices to show that
O(d)\triangleright\partial_{i}(d)=\partial_{i}(d)
. By Lemma
4.4
,

d=O(d)\triangleright d=\big(O(d)\triangleright d_{0},(O(d)\triangleleft d_{0})\triangleright d_{1},\dots,(O(d)\triangleleft(d_{0}\dots d_{q-1}))\triangleright d_{q}\big).

Hence,
\big(O(d)\triangleleft(d_{0}\dots d_{j-1})\big)\triangleright d_{j}=d_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq q
. In particular, for
i\leq q
, we have

(O(d)\triangleleft d_{0}\dots d_{i-2})\triangleright(d_{i-1}d_{i})=\big(O(d)\triangleleft d_{0}\dots d_{i-2})\triangleright d_{i-1}\big)\big((O(d)\triangleleft d_{0}\dots d_{i-1})\triangleright d_{i}\big)=d_{i-1}d_{i}.

Thus for
i\leq q
, we have

\displaystyle O(d)\triangleright\partial_{i}(d)

\displaystyle=\big(O(d)\triangleright d_{0},\dots,(O(d)\triangleleft d_{0}\dots d_{i-3})\triangleright d_{i-2},(O(d)\triangleleft d_{0}\dots d_{i-2})\triangleright(d_{i-1}d_{i}),

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad(O(d)\triangleleft d_{0}\dots d_{i})\triangleright d_{i+1},\dots,(O(d)\triangleleft d_{0}\dots d_{q-1})\triangleright d_{q}\big)

\displaystyle=(d_{0},\ldots,d_{i-2},d_{i-1}d_{i},d_{i+1},\ldots,d_{q})=\partial_{i}(d).

When
i=q+1
,
O(d)\triangleright\partial_{q+1}(d)=\partial_{q+1}(O(d)\triangleright d)=\partial_{q+1}(d)
; and when
i=0
,

\displaystyle(O(d)\triangleleft d_{0})\triangleright\partial_{0}(d)

\displaystyle=(O(d)\triangleleft d_{0})\triangleright(d_{1},d_{2},\dots,d_{q})

\displaystyle=\big((O(d)\triangleright d)_{1},(O(d)\triangleright d)_{2},\dots,(O(d)\triangleright d)_{q}\big)=(d_{1},\dots,d_{q})=\partial_{0}(d).\qed

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
be a matched pair with
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{D}^{0}\times\mathbb{Z}
. For

p\in\{0,1\}
, let
d^{1}_{p,q}\colon E^{vh,1}_{p,q+1}\to E^{vh,1}_{p,q}
,
q\geq 0
, be
the differentials in the first sheet of the spectral sequence
E^{vh}_{p,q}

of Corollary
5.18
. Let
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}
be as in
Proposition
6.6
. For
0\leq i\leq q
,
let
\partial_{i}\colon\mathcal{D}^{q+1}\to\mathcal{D}^{q}
be the face map
of (
4.1
), and let
\rho_{i}\colon\Per(\mathcal{D}^{q})\to\mathbb{Z}
be as defined in
Lemma
6.7
. Define

\displaystyle\Delta_{0,q}{}

\displaystyle:\textstyle\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\backslash u\mathcal{D}^{q+1})\to\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\backslash u\mathcal{D}^{q})\quad\text{ and}

\displaystyle\Delta_{1,q}{}

\displaystyle:\textstyle\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\backslash\Per(u\mathcal{D}^{q+1}))\to\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\backslash\Per(u\mathcal{D}^{q}))

by

\Delta_{0,q}([\![d]\!])=\sum^{q}_{i=0}(-1)^{i}[\![\partial_{i}(d)]\!],\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Delta_{1,q}([\![d]\!])=\sum^{q}_{i=0}(-1)^{i}\rho_{i}(d)[\![\partial_{i}(d)]\!].

Then
\alpha_{p}\circ\Delta_{p,q}=d^{1}_{p,q}\circ\alpha_{p}
for
p=0,1

and all
q\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular,
E^{vh,2}_{0,\bullet}
is isomorphic to
the homology of the chain complex
(\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\backslash u\mathcal{D}^{\bullet}),\Delta_{0,\bullet})
, and
E^{vh,2}_{1,\bullet}
is
isomorphic to the homology of the chain complex
(\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\backslash\Per(u\mathcal{D}^{\bullet})),\Delta_{1,\bullet})
.

Proof.

To see that
\alpha_{0}\circ\Delta_{0,q}=d^{1}_{0,q}\circ\alpha_{0}
, we use that
d_{0,q}^{v}=d_{0,q}^{1}
to compute:

\displaystyle d^{1}_{0,q}(\alpha_{0}([\![d]\!]))=d^{v}_{0,q}([r(d);d])+\im(d^{h}_{0,q+1})=\alpha_{0}(\Delta_{0,q}([\![d]\!])).

To see that
\alpha_{1}\circ\Delta_{1,q}=d^{1}_{1,q}\circ\alpha_{1}
, we first claim that for
d\in\Per(\mathcal{D}^{q})
and
n\geq 0
,

(6.12)

[n;d]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q+1})=\sum^{n-1}_{j=0}[1;j\triangleright d]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q+1})\in H^{h}_{1,q+1}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}).

We argue by induction. The case
n=0
is trivial:
[0;[\![d]\!]]
is a sum of degenerate chains. Suppose inductively that (
6.12
) holds for
n
. We calculate, using (
6.12
) at the third equality:

\displaystyle 0+\im(d^{h}_{1,q+1})

\displaystyle=d^{h}_{1,q+1}[1,n;d]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q+1})

\displaystyle=[n;d]-[n+1;d]+[1;n\triangleright d]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q+1})

\displaystyle=\sum^{n-1}_{j=0}[1;j\triangleright d]-[n+1;d]+[1;n\triangleright d]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q+1})

\displaystyle=\sum^{n}_{j=0}[1;j\triangleright d]-[n+1;d]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q+1}),

and rearranging gives (
6.12
).

Since
[\![d]\!]=\{j\triangleright d\colon 0\leq j\leq O(d)-1\}
,
Equation (
6.12
) gives

\alpha_{1}([\![d]\!])=\sum_{d^{\prime}\in[\![d]\!]}[1;d^{\prime}]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q+1})=\sum^{O(d)-1}_{j=0}[1;j\triangleright d]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q+1})=[O(d);d].

Using this at the first line, we calculate:

\displaystyle d^{1}_{1,q}(\alpha_{1}([\![d]\!]))

\displaystyle=d^{1}_{1,q}([O(d);d])+\im(d^{h}_{1,q})

\displaystyle=[O(d)\triangleleft d_{0};\partial_{0}(d)]+\sum^{q+1}_{i=1}(-1)^{i}[O(d);\partial_{i}(d)]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q})

(6.13)

\displaystyle=\sum^{q+1}_{i=0}(-1)^{i}[\rho_{i}(d)O(\partial_{i}(d));\partial_{i}(d)]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q}).

For any
d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{q}
and any
n\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle[nO(d^{\prime});d^{\prime}]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q})

\displaystyle=\sum^{nO(d^{\prime})-1}_{j=0}[1;j\triangleright d^{\prime}]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q})

\displaystyle=\sum^{n-1}_{k=0}\sum^{O(d^{\prime})-1}_{j=0}[1;j\triangleright(kO(d^{\prime})\triangleright d^{\prime})]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q})

\displaystyle=n\sum^{O(d^{\prime})-1}_{j=0}[1;j\triangleright d^{\prime}]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q})=n[O(d^{\prime});d^{\prime}]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q}).

Applying this to each term of (
6.13
), we obtain

\displaystyle d^{1}_{1,q}(\alpha_{1}([\![d]\!]))

\displaystyle=\sum^{q+1}_{i=0}(-1)^{i}\rho_{i}(d)[O(\partial_{i}(d));\partial_{i}(d)]+\im(d^{h}_{1,q})

\displaystyle=\sum^{q+1}_{i=0}(-1)^{i}\rho_{i}(d)\alpha_{1}([\![\partial_{i}(d)]\!])=\alpha_{1}(\Delta_{1,q}([\![d]\!])).

The remaining statements follow.
∎

6.4.
Graphs of odometers

Here, we apply Proposition
6.8
and Theorem
5.3
to the following class of examples
generalising the odometer action.

Set-up 6.9
.

Let
E
be a finite directed graph, and let
p\colon E^{1}\to\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}

be a function. Define
F=(F^{0},F^{1},r,s)
by
F^{0}=E^{0}
,
F^{1}=\{(e,i)\colon e\in E^{1},i\in\mathbb{Z}/p(e)\mathbb{Z}\}
,
r(e,i)=r(e)
, and
s(e,i)=s(e)
. We write
+_{p}
for
the group operation on
\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\mathcal{G}\coloneqq E^{0}\times\mathbb{Z}
. We obtain a self-similar action of
\mathcal{G}
on the
1
-graph
F^{*}
(in the sense of Definition
3.33
) by
the unique possible extension of the formulae

(r(e),1)\triangleright(e,i)=(e,i+_{p(e)}1)\quad\text{ and }\quad(r(e),1)\triangleleft(e,i)=\begin{cases}(s(e),1)&\text{ if $i=p(e)-1$}\\
(s(e),0)&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
E^{0}=\{v\}
,
E^{1}=\{e\}
, and
p(e)=2
, then
(\mathcal{G},F^{*})
is the binary odometer.

Extend
p
to a functor
p\colon E^{*}\to\mathbb{N}^{\times}
. Then

\Theta\colon F^{*}\to\{(\mu,i)\mid\mu\in E^{*},\,i\in\mathbb{Z}/p(\mu)\mathbb{Z}\}
given by

\Theta((e_{1},m_{1})(e_{2},m_{2})\cdots(e_{k},m_{k}))=\big(e_{1}e_{2}\cdots e_{k},\sum^{k}_{j=1}m_{j}p(e_{1}\cdots e_{j-1})\big).

is a bijection. Identifying
F^{*}
with
\{(\mu,i)\mid\mu\in E^{*},\,i\in\mathbb{Z}/p(\mu)\mathbb{Z}\}
via
\Theta
, and writing
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{Z}
for the floor function
\lfloor x\rfloor=\max\{n\in\mathbb{Z}:n\leq x\}
, we have

(r(\mu),a)\triangleright(\mu,m)=(\mu,a+_{p(\mu)}m)\quad\text{ and }\quad(r(\mu),a)\triangleleft(\mu,m)=\big(s(\mu),\lfloor(a+m)/p(\mu)\rfloor\big)

(in the second formula,
m
is regarded as an element of
\{0,\dots p(\mu)-1\}
and the addition
a+m
is computed in
\mathbb{Z}
).
It is helpful to
keep in mind the special case that

(6.14)

a\triangleright(\mu,0)=(\mu,a\bmod p(\mu)),\qquad\text{ and }\qquad a\triangleleft(\mu,0)=\lfloor a/p(\mu)\rfloor.

Remark 6.10
.

The self-similar actions of Set-Up
6.9
are faithful self-similar actions as in Definition
3.30
. They are also length-preserving matched pairs as in Subsection
6.1
.

We use the symbols
\lambda,\mu,\nu
for paths in
E
and
\xi,\eta,\zeta
for paths in
F
. So an element of
F^{*}
might
be written as
\xi=(\mu,m)
. We write
\overline{p}\colon F^{*}\to\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
for the map
\overline{p}(\mu,m)=p(\mu)
.

Lemma 6.11
.

In the situation of Set-up
6.9
, we have

\Per(uF^{*q})=uF^{*q}
for each
u\in F^{0}
and
q\in E^{0}
. For each
u\in E^{0}
the map
(\mu_{0},\dots,\mu_{q-1})\mapsto\mathcal{G}_{u}\triangleright((\mu_{0},0),\dots,(\mu_{q-1},0))
induces an
isomorphism
\kappa_{q}\colon\mathbb{Z}uE^{*q}\to\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{G}_{u}\backslash uF^{*q})
. The functions
O,\rho_{i}\colon(F^{*})^{q+1}\to\mathbb{Z}
satisfy

(6.15)

O(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{q})=p(\xi_{0}\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{q})\quad\text{and}\quad\rho_{i}(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{q})=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $0\leq i<q$}\\
\overline{p}(\xi_{q})&\text{ if $i=q$}.\end{cases}

Proof.

For
p_{0},\ldots,p_{q}>0
, the odometer
action
\operatorname{Od}
of
\mathbb{Z}
on
\prod^{q}_{i=0}(\mathbb{Z}/p_{i}\mathbb{Z})
is transitive, so the order of any point under
\operatorname{Od}
is
\prod_{i}p_{i}
. For
u\in E^{0}
and
\mu=(\mu_{0},\dots,\mu_{q-1})\in E^{*}u
, the action of
\mathcal{G}_{u}
on
\{((\mu_{0},m_{0}),\dots,(\mu_{q},m_{q}))\mid m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}/p(\mu_{i})\mathbb{Z}\}
is conjugate to
this odometer with
p_{i}=p(\mu_{i})
. So each
O(\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{q})=\prod^{q}_{i=0}\overline{p}(\xi_{i})=\overline{p}(\xi_{0}\cdots\xi_{q})
, we have
\Per(uF^{*q})=uF^{*q}
, and
(\mu_{0},\dots,\mu_{q-1})\mapsto\mathcal{G}_{u}\triangleright((\mu_{0},0),\dots,(\mu_{q-1},0))
is a bijection
uE^{*q}\to\mathcal{G}_{u}\backslash uF^{*q}
.

For the
\rho_{i}
, observe that if
i\geq 1
, then writing
\partial^{i}(\xi)=(\eta_{0},\dots,\eta_{q-1})
, we have
\eta_{0}\cdots\eta_{q-1}=\xi_{0}\cdots\xi_{q}
if
i\not=q
and
\eta_{0}\cdots\eta_{q-1}=\xi_{0}\cdots\xi_{q-1}
if
i=q
. Hence (
6.15
) implies that

\rho_{i}(\xi)=O(\xi)/O(\partial_{i}(\xi))=1
if
i<q
, and
\rho_{q}(\xi)=O(\xi)/O(\partial_{q}(\xi))=\overline{p}(\xi_{q})
.

It remains to calculate
\rho_{0}(\xi)
. Since
\operatorname{Od}
is transitive,
\id\times\operatorname{Od}
is transitive on
\{(\mu_{0},\dots,\mu_{q})\}\times\prod^{q}_{i=0}\mathbb{Z}/p(\mu_{i})\mathbb{Z}
. So it suffices to show that
\xi=((\mu_{0},0),(\mu_{1},0),\dots,(\mu_{q},0))
satisfies
\rho_{0}(\xi)=1
. Applying (
6.14
), with
a=O(\xi)=p(\xi_{0}\cdots\xi_{q})
, gives

O(\xi)\triangleleft(\mu_{0},0)=\lfloor O(\xi)/p(\mu_{0})\rfloor=\lfloor p(\mu_{0}\cdots\mu_{q})/p(\mu_{0})\rfloor=p(\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{q})=O(\partial_{0}(\xi)).\qed

Lemma 6.12
.

With Set-up
6.9
let
\kappa_{q}\colon\mathbb{Z}uE^{*q}\to\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{G}_{u}\backslash uF^{*q})
be the isomorphism of Lemma
6.11
. For each

0\leq i\leq q
, let
\partial_{i}\colon E^{*(q+1)}\to E^{*q}
be the face map
of (
4.1
). Let
\Delta_{1,q}
be as in
Proposition
6.8
, and define

\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,q}{}\colon\textstyle\bigoplus_{u\in E^{0}}\mathbb{Z}uE^{*(q+1)}\to\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathbb{Z}uE^{*q}

by

(6.16)

\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,q}(\mu)=\Big(\sum^{q-1}_{i=0}(-1)^{i}\partial_{i}(\mu)\Big)+(-1)^{q}p(\mu_{q})\partial_{q}(\mu).

Then
\kappa_{q}\circ\Delta_{1,q}=\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,q}\circ\kappa_{q+1}
for all
q
.

Proof.

This follows directly from (
6.16
) and Lemma
6.11
.
∎

To compute homology for Set-up
6.9
, we must compute
\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1}\big(\bigoplus_{u\in E^{0}}\mathbb{Z}uE^{*2}\big)\subseteq\bigoplus_{u\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathbb{Z}uE^{*}
.

Lemma 6.13
.

In the situation of Set-up
6.9
, we have

\mathbb{Z}E^{*}=\mathbb{Z}E^{1}+\im(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1})
, and

\im(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1})\cap\mathbb{Z}E^{1}=\{0\}
. In particular,
\mathbb{Z}E^{*}\cong\mathbb{Z}E^{1}\oplus\im(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1})
.

Proof.

Since
\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,0}(\mathbb{Z}E^{0})=0
and
\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1}(\mathbb{Z}(E^{0}*E^{*}))=\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1}(\mathbb{Z}(E^{*}*E^{0}))=\mathbb{Z}E^{0}
, it suffices to show that
\mathbb{Z}E^{\geq 1}=\mathbb{Z}E^{1}+\im(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1}|_{\mathbb{Z}E^{\geq 1}})
and

\im(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1}|_{\mathbb{Z}E^{\geq 1}})\cap\mathbb{Z}E^{1}=\{0\}
.

Recall that for
\mu\in E^{\geq 1}
and
1\leq i\leq|\mu|
, the elements
\mu^{[0,i-1]}\in E^{i-1}
,
\mu^{i}\in E^{1}
and
\mu^{[i,|\mu|]}\in E^{|\mu|-i}
are defined implicitly by
\mu=\mu^{[0,i-1]}\mu^{i}\mu^{[i,|\mu|]}
. Let
\widetilde{\Delta}\coloneqq\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1}
. To see that
\mathbb{Z}E^{\geq 1}=\mathbb{Z}E^{1}+\im(\widetilde{\Delta}|_{\mathbb{Z}E^{\geq 1}})
, it suffices to show that for
\mu\in E^{*}\setminus E^{0}
,

(6.17)

\mu\in\Big(\sum^{|\mu|}_{i=1}p(\mu^{[i,|\mu|]}){\mu^{i}}\Big)-\widetilde{\Delta}({\mu^{[0,|\mu|-1]},\mu^{|\mu|}})+\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\{\widetilde{\Delta}({\alpha,\beta})\colon|\alpha\beta|<|\mu|\}.

in
\mathbb{Z}E^{*}
.
We induct on
|\mu|
. If
|\mu|=1
then
\sum^{|\mu|}_{i=1}p(\mu^{[i,|\mu|]}){\mu^{i}}=p(s(\mu))\mu=\mu
and (
6.17
) is trivial. Now suppose that (
6.17
) holds for
|\mu|\leq n
and fix
\mu\in E^{n+1}
. Write
\mu=\nu e
with
e\in E^{1}
. Then
e=\mu^{[n,n+1]}=\mu^{n+1}
, and
\mu^{[i,n+1]}=\nu^{[i,n]}e
and
\nu^{i}=\mu^{i}
for
i\leq n
. We calculate (in
\mathbb{Z}E^{*}
):

(6.18)

\displaystyle\mu=-\widetilde{\Delta}({\nu,e})+e+p(e)\nu=-\widetilde{\Delta}({\nu,e})+p(\mu^{[n,n+1]}){\mu^{n+1}}+p(\mu^{n+1})\nu.

By the inductive hypothesis,

\displaystyle p(\mu^{n+1})\nu

\displaystyle\in p(\mu^{n+1})\Big(\Big(\sum^{n}_{i=1}p(\nu^{[i,n]}){\nu^{i}}\Big)-\widetilde{\Delta}({\nu^{[0,n-1]}},\nu^{n})+\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\{\widetilde{\Delta}({\alpha,\beta})\colon|\alpha\beta|<n\}\Big)

\displaystyle\subseteq\sum^{n}_{i=1}p(\mu^{n+1})p(\nu^{[i,n]}){\nu^{i}}+\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\{\widetilde{\Delta}({\alpha,\beta})\colon|\alpha\beta|<n\}.

Since
p
is multiplicative and each
\nu^{[i,n]}\mu^{n+1}=\mu^{[i,n+1]}
, we obtain

p(\mu^{n+1})\nu\in\sum^{n}_{i=1}p(\mu^{[i,n+1]}){\mu^{i}}+\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\{\widetilde{\Delta}({\alpha,\beta})\colon|\alpha\beta|<n+1\}.

Substituting this into (
6.18
)
completes the induction, proving the first statement.

For
\im(\widetilde{\Delta}|_{\mathbb{Z}E^{\geq 1}})\cap\mathbb{Z}E^{1}=\{0\}
,
fix
(\mu,\nu),(\eta,\zeta)\in E^{\geq 1}*E^{\geq 1}
with
\mu\nu=\eta\zeta
. We claim that

(6.19)

\widetilde{\Delta}({\mu,\nu})-\widetilde{\Delta}({\eta,\zeta})\in\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\{\widetilde{\Delta}({\alpha,\beta})\colon|\alpha\beta|<|\mu\nu|\}.

We first show that if
\mu,\nu\in E^{*}\setminus E^{0}
and
\mu\nu=\lambda
, then

(6.20)

\widetilde{\Delta}({\mu,\nu})\in-{\lambda}+\sum^{|\lambda|}_{i=1}p(\lambda^{[i,|\lambda|]}){\lambda^{i}}+\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\{\widetilde{\Delta}({\alpha,\beta})\colon|\alpha\beta|<|\lambda|\}.

For this, we calculate, applying (
6.17
) twice at the
second step,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}({\mu,\nu})

\displaystyle=\nu-{\mu\nu}+p(\nu)\mu

\displaystyle\in-{\mu\nu}-\widetilde{\Delta}(\nu^{[0,|\nu|-1]},\nu^{|\nu|})+\sum^{|\nu|}_{i=1}p(\nu^{[i,|\nu|]}){\nu^{i}}-p(\nu)\Big(\widetilde{\Delta}(\mu^{[0,|\mu|-1]},\mu^{|\mu|})+\sum^{|\mu|}_{i=1}p(\mu^{[i,|\mu|]}){\mu^{i}}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad{}+\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\big\{\widetilde{\Delta}({\alpha,\beta})\colon|\alpha\beta|<\max\{|\mu|,|\nu|\}\big\}.

Since each
\mu^{[i,|\mu|]}\nu=(\mu\nu)^{[i,|\mu\nu|]}=\lambda^{[i,|\lambda|]}

and since
p
is multiplicative, this gives

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}({\mu,\nu})

\displaystyle\in-{\mu\nu}+\sum^{|\lambda|}_{i=1}p(\lambda^{[i,|\lambda|]}){\lambda^{i}}+-\widetilde{\Delta}(\nu^{[0,|\nu|-1]},\nu^{|\nu|})-\widetilde{\Delta}(p(\nu)\mu^{[0,|\mu|-1]},\mu^{|\mu|})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad{}+\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\big\{\widetilde{\Delta}({\alpha,\beta})\colon|\alpha\beta|\leq\max\{|\mu|,|\nu|\}\big\}.

Since
|\mu|,|\nu|<|\lambda|
, we
obtain (
6.20
).
Since the terms
-{\lambda}+\sum^{|\lambda|}_{i=1}p(\lambda^{[i,|\lambda|-1]}){\lambda^{i}}
in the right-hand side
of (
6.20
) depend only on the product
\mu\nu
, we obtain (
6.19
).

Now, we suppose that
\im(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1}|_{\mathbb{Z}E^{\geq 1}})\cap\mathbb{Z}E^{1}\neq\{0\}
and derive a contradiction. Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
be minimal such that there exists
a\in\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\{{(\mu,\nu)}\colon|\mu\nu|\leq l\}
with

\widetilde{\Delta}(a)\in\mathbb{Z}E^{1}\setminus\{0\}
. Write
a=\sum a_{\mu,\nu}(\mu,\nu)
. For each
(\mu,\nu)
such
that
|\mu\nu|=l
and
0\neq a_{\mu,\nu}\in\mathbb{Z}
, Equation (
6.19
) gives

\widetilde{\Delta}\Big(a_{\mu,\nu}\big((\mu^{1},(\mu\nu)^{[1,l]})-{(\mu,\nu)}\big)\Big)\in\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\{\widetilde{\Delta}({\alpha,\beta})\colon|\alpha\beta|<l\}.

Hence,

a^{\prime}\coloneqq a+\sum_{|\mu\nu|=l}a_{\mu,\nu}\big((\mu^{1},(\mu\nu)^{[1,l]})-{(\mu,\nu)}\big)

satisfies
\widetilde{\Delta}(a)-\widetilde{\Delta}(a^{\prime})\in\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\{\widetilde{\Delta}({\alpha,\beta})\colon|\alpha\beta|<l\}
.
Fix
b\in\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\{{(\alpha,\beta)}\colon|\alpha\beta|<l\}
such that

\widetilde{\Delta}(a)=\widetilde{\Delta}(a^{\prime})+\widetilde{\Delta}(b)=\widetilde{\Delta}(a^{\prime}+b)
. Let
a^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq a^{\prime}+b
; by construction,
a^{\prime\prime}\in\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\{{(\mu,\nu)}\colon|\mu\nu|\leq l\}
.

Write
a^{\prime}=\sum a^{\prime}_{\mu,\nu}(\mu,\nu)
. Then
a^{\prime}_{\mu,\nu}=0
for all
(\mu,\nu)
such that

|\mu\nu|=l
and
|\mu|>1
. Write
b=\sum b_{\mu,\nu}(\mu,\nu)
. Then
b_{\mu,\nu}=0
for all
\mu,\nu
with
|\mu\nu|=l
. Hence
a^{\prime\prime}_{\mu,\nu}=0
whenever

|\mu\nu|=l
and
|\mu|>1
. We have
\widetilde{\Delta}(a^{\prime\prime})=\widetilde{\Delta}(a)\in\mathbb{Z}E^{1}\setminus\{0\}
, and since
l
is minimal there
exist
\nu\in E^{l-1}
and
e\in E^{1}r(\nu)
such that
a^{\prime\prime}_{e,\nu}\not=0
.
We have

(6.21)

\widetilde{\Delta}(a^{\prime\prime})_{e\nu}=\sum_{\alpha\in E^{*}r(e)}a^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha,e\nu}-\sum_{\eta\zeta=e\nu}a^{\prime\prime}_{\eta,\zeta}+\sum_{\tau\in s(e)E^{*}}p(\tau)a^{\prime\prime}_{e\nu,\tau}.

By construction of
a^{\prime\prime}
, the only nonzero term in (
6.21
) is
-a^{\prime\prime}_{e,\nu}
in the middle sum, so

\widetilde{\Delta}(a^{\prime\prime})_{e\nu}=-a^{\prime\prime}_{e,\nu}\not=0,

which contradicts that
\widetilde{\Delta}(a^{\prime\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}E^{1}
.
∎

Define
M_{s}\in M_{E^{0},E^{1}}(\mathbb{Z})
by
M_{s}(v,e)=\delta_{v,s(e)}
, regarded as a group homomorphism from
\mathbb{Z}E^{1}
to
\mathbb{Z}E^{0}
. Similarly, define
M_{r}\in M_{E^{0},E^{1}}(\mathbb{Z})
by
M_{r}(v,e)=\delta_{v,r(e)}
. Let
P\in M_{E^{1}}(\mathbb{Z})
be the diagonal matrix
P(e,f)=\delta_{e,f}p(e)
. Finally, define
M\colon\mathbb{Z}E^{1}\to\mathbb{Z}E^{0}
by

(6.22)

M\coloneqq M_{r}P-M_{s}.

In matrix form,
M\in M_{E^{0},E^{1}}(\mathbb{Z})
is given by

M(v,e)=p(e)\delta_{v,r(e)}-\delta_{v,s(e)}=\begin{cases}p(e)&\text{ if $v=r(e)$ and $s(e)\not=r(e)$}\\
-1&\text{ if $v=s(e)$ and $s(e)\not=r(e)$}\\
p(e)-1&\text{ if $v=r(e)=s(e)$}\\
0&\text{ if $v\not\in\{r(e),s(e)\}$.}\end{cases}

Proposition 6.14
.

With Set-up
6.9
, let
M\colon\mathbb{Z}E^{1}\to\mathbb{Z}E^{0}
be the homomorphism (
6.22
). The spectral sequence of Corollary
5.18
satisfies
E_{i,j}^{vh,2}=0
whenever
\max\{i,j\}\geq 2
,

\displaystyle E_{0,1}^{vh,2}\cong H_{1}(E),\quad E_{1,1}^{vh,2}\cong\ker(M),\quad E_{0,0}^{vh,2}\cong H_{0}(E),\quad\text{ and }\quad E_{1,0}^{vh,2}\cong\coker(M).

Proof.

Lemma
6.11
gives
\Per((F^{*})^{q})=(F^{*})^{q}
, so
Proposition
6.8
implies that
E^{vh,2}_{p,\bullet}

is isomorphic to the homology of the chain complex
\big(\bigoplus_{u\in E^{0}}\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{G}_{u}\backslash uF^{*\bullet}),\Delta_{p,\bullet}\big)
for
p=0,1
.
Lemma
6.12
gives isomorphisms
\kappa_{q}\colon E^{*q}\to\mathcal{G}\backslash F^{*q}
intertwining the
\Delta_{0,\bullet}
with the
categorical-homology boundary maps for
E
, giving the descriptions of

E_{0,\bullet}^{vh,2}
.

Lemma
6.12
yields an isomorphism
\kappa_{\bullet}\colon\big(\bigoplus_{u\in E^{0}}\mathbb{Z}uE^{*\bullet},\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,\bullet}\big)\to\big(\bigoplus_{u\in E^{0}}\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{G}_{u}\backslash uF^{*\bullet}),\Delta_{1,\bullet}\big)
of chain
complexes induced by the
\kappa_{q}
. So

E_{1,\bullet}^{vh,2}\cong H_{\bullet}\big(\bigoplus_{u\in E^{0}}\mathbb{Z}uE^{*\bullet},\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,\bullet}\big)
.

We have
\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,0}(\mu)=p(\mu){r(\mu)}-{s(\mu)}
, giving

E_{1,0}^{vh,2}=\mathbb{Z}E^{0}/\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\{p(\mu){r(\mu)}-{s(\mu)}\colon\mu\in E^{*}\}.

Fix
\mu\in E^{*}
. The telescoping identity

p(\mu){r(\mu)}-{s(\mu)}=\sum^{|\mu|}_{i=1}p(\mu^{i+1}\dots\mu^{|\mu|})\big(p(\mu^{i}){r(\mu^{i})}-{s(\mu^{i})}\big),

gives
\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\{p(\mu){r(\mu)}-{s(\mu)}\colon\mu\in E^{*}\}\subseteq\lsp_{\mathbb{Z}}\{p(e){r(e)}-{s(e)}\colon e\in E^{1}\}
. The
reverse containment is trivial. Since each
p(e)r(e)-{s(e)}=Me
, we deduce that
E_{1,0}^{vh,2}=\coker(M)
.

It remains to calculate
E_{1,1}^{vh,2}\cong\ker(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,0})/\im(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1})
.
Clearly,
(\ker(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,0})\cap\mathbb{Z}E^{1})+\im(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1})\subseteq\ker(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,0})
. Conversely, if
a\in\ker(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,0})
, then Lemma
6.13
says that
a=a^{\prime}+x
for some
a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}E^{1}
and
x\in\im(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1})
. Then
\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,0}(a^{\prime})=\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,0}(a-x)=0
, so
a^{\prime}\in\ker(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,0})\cap\mathbb{Z}E^{1}
. Hence,

\frac{\ker(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,0})}{\im(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1})}=\frac{(\ker(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,0})\cap\mathbb{Z}E^{1})+\im(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1})}{\im(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1})}\cong\frac{\ker(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,0})\cap\mathbb{Z}E^{1}}{\im(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,1})\cap\mathbb{Z}E^{1}}=\ker(\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,0}|_{\mathbb{Z}E^{1}}).

The restriction of

\widetilde{\Delta}_{1,0}
to
\mathbb{Z}E^{1}
is
M
, so

E_{1,1}^{vh,2}\cong\ker(M)
.
∎

We obtain a computation of the homology of matched pairs
(\mathcal{G},F^{*})
as in Set-up
6.9
.

Theorem 6.15
.

In the situation of Set-up
6.9
, with
M\in M_{E^{0},E^{1}}(\mathbb{Z})
as in (
6.22
),

H_{0}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})\cong H_{0}(E),\qquad\text{ and }\qquad H_{2}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})\cong\ker(M),

and there is a short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{1}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coker(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Proof.

This follows immediately from Lemma
6.3
and Proposition
6.14
.
∎

Given a finite directed graph
E
, we define
\chi(E)\coloneqq|E^{0}|-|E^{1}|
, the Euler characteristic of
E
.

Corollary 6.16
.

In the situation of Set-up
6.9
, suppose that
vE^{*}w\not=\varnothing

for all
v,w\in E^{0}
, and that
E^{1}\not=\varnothing
.

(i)

If
p(e)=1
for all
e\in E^{1}
, then

\displaystyle H_{0}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})\cong\mathbb{Z},

\displaystyle H_{1}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2-\chi(E)},\quad\text{ and}

\displaystyle H_{2}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{1-\chi(E)}.

(ii)

If
p(e)>1
for some
e\in E^{1}
, then
\coker(M)
is a finite cyclic
group,

H_{0}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})\cong\mathbb{Z}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad H_{2}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{-\chi(E)},

and there is a short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}^{1-\chi(E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{1}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coker(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0;}

if
\gcd\big\{p(\mu)-p(\nu)\colon\mu,\nu\in E^{*},s(\mu)=s(\nu)\text{ and
}r(\mu)=r(\nu)\big\}=1
, then
\coker(M)=0
and
H_{1}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{1-\chi(E)}
.

Proof.

By
[
Mas91
, p.194]
(immediately after
Theorem 3.4),
H_{0}(E)
is the free abelian group generated by the connected components of
E
. Since
vE^{*}w\not=\varnothing

for all
v,w\in E^{0}
, this is
a singleton. So
H_{0}(E)=\mathbb{Z}
. This and
[
Mas91
, Theorem 3.4]
give
H_{1}(E)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{1-\chi(E)}
. We must compute
\ker(M)
and
\coker(M)
.

(i)
Suppose that
p(e)=1

for all
E
. Lemma
6.11
gives
\rho_{i}\equiv 1
for all
i
, and
\Delta_{1,q}=\Delta_{0,q}
for all
q
. So

\ker(M)\cong E^{vh,2}_{1,1}\cong E^{vh,2}_{0,1}\cong H_{1}(E)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{1-\chi(E)}
and
\coker(M)\cong E^{vh,2}_{1,0}\cong E^{vh,2}_{0,0}\cong H_{0}(E)\cong\mathbb{Z}
. In particular,
\coker(M)
is free abelian, so the extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{1}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coker(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

of Theorem
6.15
splits, giving the desired formulae for
H_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})
.

(ii)
Now suppose that
p(e)>1
for some
e
. By assumption, there exists
\mu\in s(e)E^{*}r(e)
, and
p(e\mu)=p(e)p(\mu)>1
. For
\nu\in E^{*}
, we have

p(\nu){r(\nu)}-{s(\nu)}=\sum^{|\nu|}_{i=1}p(\nu^{i+1}\cdots\nu^{|\nu|})(p(\nu^{i}){r(\nu^{i})}-{s(\nu^{i})})\in\im(M)
. In particular,
(p(e\mu)-1)r(e)=p(e\mu){r(e\mu)}-{s(e\mu)}\in\im(M)
, and so

{r(e)}+\im(M)
has finite order in
\coker(M)
.

Fix
w\in E^{0}
. By assumption, there exists
\nu\in r(e)E^{*}w
and so
w+\im(M)=w+p(\nu){r(\nu)}-{s(\nu)}+\im(M)=p(\mu){r(e)}+\im(M)
. So
\coker(M)=\mathbb{Z}{r(e)}+\im(M)
is
a finite cyclic group. Hence,
\operatorname{rank}(\im(M))=\operatorname{rank}(\mathbb{Z}E^{0})=|E^{0}|
, and Rank-Nullity
for
\mathbb{Z}
-modules gives
\operatorname{rank}(\ker(M))=\operatorname{rank}(\mathbb{Z}E^{1})-\operatorname{rank}(\mathbb{Z}E^{0})
. Since
\ker(M)
is a subgroup of a free
abelian group, it is free abelian, so
\ker(M)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{-\chi(E)}
. The formulae for
H_{0}
and
H_{2}
and the exact sequence
involving
H_{1}
now follow from Theorem
6.15
.

Finally, suppose that
\gcd\big\{p(\mu)-p(\nu)\colon\mu,\nu\in E^{*},s(\mu)=s(\nu)\text{ and }r(\mu)=r(\nu)\big\}=1
. As above,
a\coloneqq{r(e)}+\im(M)
generates
\coker(M)
. So it suffices
to show that
O(a)
divides
p(\mu)-p(\nu)
whenever

s(\mu)=s(\nu)
and
r(\mu)=r(\nu)
.
Fix
v,w\in E^{0}
and

\mu,\nu\in vE^{*}w
. We have
(p(\mu)-p(\nu))v=(p(\mu){r(\mu)}-{s(\mu)})-(p(\nu){r(\nu)}-{s(\nu)})\in\im(M)
. Fix
\alpha\in vE^{*}r(e)
. Then
r(e)+\im(M)=r(e)+(p(\alpha){r(\alpha)}-{s(\alpha)})+\im(M)=p(\alpha)v+\im(M)
. In particular,
(p(\mu)-p(\nu))r(e)+\im(M)=p(\alpha)(p(\mu)-p(\nu))v+\im(M)=0+\im(M)
. So
O(v+\im(M))
divides
p(\mu)-p(\nu)
.
∎

Remark 6.17
.

The situation when
p(e)=1
for all
e
in Corollary
6.16
boils down to
\mathcal{G}\bowtie F^{*}=\mathbb{Z}\times E^{*}
, so Corollary
6.16
(i)
is a nice reality check: it says that
H^{\bowtie}_{p}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})=\bigoplus_{i+j=p}H_{i}(\mathbb{Z})\otimes H_{j}(E^{*})
, in the spirit of the usual Künneth formula.

Example 6.18
.

Suppose that
E
is the directed graph with a single vertex
v
and a single edge
e
, so
\chi(E)=0
. Fix
p(e)\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
and form the matched pair
(\mathcal{G},F^{*})
of Set-up
6.9
. Then
H_{0}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})\cong H_{0}(E)\cong\mathbb{Z}
. The map
M\colon\mathbb{Z}E^{1}\to\mathbb{Z}E^{0}
is
\times(p(e)-1)
, so we obtain an exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{1}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}/(p(e)-1)\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

If
p(e)=1
, then
H_{1}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
H_{2}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})\cong\mathbb{Z}
; if
p(e)=2
(the binary odometer), then
H_{1}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})\cong\mathbb{Z}
and
H_{2}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})=0
.

For
p(e)>2
the group cohomology
H^{2}(\mathbb{Z}/(p(e)-1)\mathbb{Z};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}/(p(e)-1)\mathbb{Z}
, so the exact sequence in Corollary
6.16

(ii)
does not completely determine
{H_{1}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},F^{*})}
.

7.
Twisted
C^{*}
-algebras of self-similar groupoid actions on
k
-graphs

We give two constructions of a twisted
C^{*}
-algebra from a self-similar action of a groupoid on a
k
-graph as in Definition
3.33
. This is a matched pair consisting of a groupoid and a
k
-graph in which the left action preserves the degree map. Recall from Proposition
3.32
that these generalise the faithful self-similar actions of groupoids on graphs and
k
-graphs of
[
LRRW18
,
ABRW19
]
. Our self-similar actions are the examples of
[
LawV22
]
in which the generalised higher-rank
k
-graphs are
k
-graphs.

Our first construction of such a
C^{*}
-algebra is twisted by a normalised 2-cocycle in
C_{\Tot}^{2}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;\mathbb{T})
and the second is twisted by a normalised 2-cocycle in

C_{\bowtie}^{2}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;\mathbb{T})
. We show that cohomologous cocycles yield isomorphic twisted
C^{*}
-algebras, and that our two constructions are compatible via the isomorphism of cohomology groups of Corollary
5.4
. So
all possible twisted
C^{*}
-algebras arise via total 2-cycles.

We first study
C^{*}
-algebras twisted by categorical cocycles, and
establish some elementary structure theory, including a gauge-invariant uniqueness theorem.

Recall from
[
KP00
]
that a
k
-graph
\Lambda
is
row-finite
and has
no sources
if
0<|v\Lambda^{n}|<\infty
for all
v\in\Lambda^{0}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. Following
[
RS05
]
(see also
[
RSY04
, Remark 2.3]
), if
\Lambda
is a
k
-graph and
\mu,\nu\in\Lambda
we define

\MCE(\mu,\nu)\coloneqq\mu\Lambda\cap\nu\Lambda\cap\Lambda^{d(\mu)\vee d(\nu)}.

Elements of
\MCE(\mu,\nu)
are called
minimal common extensions
of
\mu
and
\nu
.

We adopt the usual conventions from the theory of
C^{*}
-algebras that homomorphisms are
*
-homomorphisms, and that ideals are closed 2-sided ideals.

7.1.
Twists by categorical cocycles

Given a normalised categorical
2
-cocycle
c\colon\mathcal{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
and
a subcategory
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
, we
write
c
for the restriction of
c
to
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{2}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{2}
.
Given a self similar action of a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
on a
k
-graph
\Lambda
, we regard
\mathcal{G}
and
\Lambda
as subsets of

\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda
; so
g\lambda=(g\triangleleft\lambda)(g\triangleright\lambda)
for
(g,\lambda)\in\mathcal{G}*\Lambda
.

Definition 7.1

(cf.
[
Yus23
,
ABRW19
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be a self-similar action of a groupoid on a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources.
Let
c\colon(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda)^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
be a normalised categorical

2
-cocycle. A
Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family
in
a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
is a function
t\colon\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda\to A
, such that

(TCK1)

t_{\zeta}t_{\eta}=\delta_{s(\zeta),r(\eta)}c(\zeta,\eta)t_{\zeta\eta}
for all
(\zeta,\eta)\in(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda)^{*2}
,

(TCK2)

t_{s(\zeta)}=t^{*}_{\zeta}t_{\zeta}
for all
\zeta\in\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda
,

(TCK3)

for all
\mu,\nu\in\Lambda
we have
t_{\mu}t_{\mu}^{*}t_{\nu}t_{\nu}^{*}=\sum_{\lambda\in\MCE(\mu,\nu)}t_{\lambda}t_{\lambda}^{*}
.

We call
t
a
Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family
if, in addition

(CK)

t_{v}=\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{n}}t_{\lambda}t^{*}_{\lambda}
for all

v\in\Lambda^{0}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
.

We write
C^{*}(t)\coloneqq C^{*}(\{t_{\zeta}\mid\zeta\in\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda\})\subseteq A
.

Remark 7.2
.

Relation
(TCK2)
for
\zeta=v\in\Lambda^{0}
is
t_{v}^{*}t_{v}=t_{v}
, so
t_{v}
is a projection. Now
(TCK2)
for any
\zeta
implies that
t_{\zeta}
is a partial isometry.

Relation
(TCK1)
implies that the
t_{v}
, for
v\in\Lambda^{0}
, are mutually orthogonal
[
KP00
, Remarks 1.6(vi)]
. So
(TCK1)
–
(TCK3)
say that
\{t_{\lambda}\colon\lambda\in\Lambda\}
is a Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\Lambda,c)
-family as in
[
SWW14
]
, and so induces a homomorphism
\iota^{t}_{\Lambda}\colon\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\Lambda,c)\to A
,
which descends to a homomorphism
\iota^{t}_{\Lambda}\colon C^{*}(\Lambda,c)\to A
if
t
is a Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family.

If
\mu,\nu\in\Lambda
satisfy
d(\mu)=d(\nu)
, then
\MCE(\mu,\nu)=\{\mu\}
if
\mu=\nu
and
\emptyset
otherwise (see
[
SWW14
, Lemma 3.2]
). So
(TCK3)
gives
t_{\mu}t_{\mu}^{*}t_{\nu}t_{\nu}^{*}=\delta_{\mu,\nu}t_{\nu}t_{\nu}^{*}
. Since
c
is normalised,
(TCK1)
implies that
t_{r(\mu)}t_{\mu}=t_{\mu}
, so
t_{\mu}t_{\mu}^{*}\leq t_{r(\mu)}
for all
\mu\in\Lambda
. Hence, as in
[
SWW14
, Remark 3.4]
, every Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family satisfies

(TCK4)

t_{v}\geq\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{n}}t_{\lambda}t^{*}_{\lambda}
for all

v\in\Lambda^{0}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
.

Relation
(TCK1)
for
g\in\mathcal{G}
gives
t_{g}t_{g^{-1}}=c(g,g^{-1})t_{r(g)}
, so

t_{g}\overline{c(g,g^{-1})}t_{g^{-1}}=t_{r(g)}
. Uniqueness of
quasi-inverses in an inverse semigroup then forces
\overline{c(g,g^{-1})}t_{g^{-1}}=t_{g}^{*}
, so
g\mapsto t_{g}
is a twisted unitary representation of
\mathcal{G}
à la
[
Ren80
]
, and so induces a homomorphism

\iota^{t}_{\mathcal{G}}\colon C^{*}(\mathcal{G},c)\to A
.

The following standard arguments
[
Spe20
,
SWW14
]
show that every
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
admits a Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger-family of nonzero partial isometries.

Example 7.3
.

By Example
3.16
and left cancellativity of
k
-graphs,
\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda
is left cancellative. Hence, for each
\zeta\in\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda
there is a partial isometry,
L_{\zeta}\in\mathcal{B}(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda))
such that

L_{\zeta}e_{\eta}=\delta_{s(\zeta),r(\eta)}c(\zeta,\eta)e_{\zeta\eta}
for all
\eta\in\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda
.
Routine calculations show that this determines a
Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family
L\colon\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda\to\mathcal{B}(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda))
.

We claim that
\{L_{\mu}L_{g}L^{*}_{\nu}\colon\mu,\nu\in\Lambda,g\in\mathcal{G}^{s(\mu)}_{s(\nu)}\}
is linearly independent. To see this fix a linear combination
a=\sum_{\mu,g,\nu}a_{\mu,g,\nu}L_{\mu}L_{g}L^{*}_{\nu}
with at least one nonzero coefficient. Fix
(\mu,g,\nu)
such that
a_{\mu,g,\nu}\not=0
and
a_{\mu^{\prime},g^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}}=0
whenever
d(\nu^{\prime})<d(\nu)
. Then
L_{\nu^{\prime}}^{*}e_{\nu}=0

whenever
a_{\mu^{\prime},g,\nu^{\prime}}\not=0
and
\nu^{\prime}\not=\nu
. Hence,
\|a\|\geq|(ae_{\nu}\mid e_{\mu g})|=|a_{\mu,g,\nu}|\not=0
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be a self-similar action of a groupoid on a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources. Let
c\colon(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda)^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
be a
normalised categorical
2
-cocycle. There is a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
generated by a Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family
t
that is universal for Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-families: if
T
is a Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family, then there is a unique homomorphism
\pi^{T}\colon\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)\to C^{*}(T)
such that
T=\pi^{T}\circ t
.

Consider the ideal
I
of
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
generated
by
\{t_{v}-\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{n}}t_{\lambda}t^{*}_{\lambda}\mid v\in\Lambda^{0}\}
. Then
s\colon\zeta\mapsto t_{\zeta}+I
is a Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family in
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)\coloneqq\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)/I
, and is
universal for Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-families: if
S
is a Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family, then there is a unique homomorphism
\pi^{S}\colon C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)\to C^{*}(S)
such that
S=\pi^{S}\circ s
.

To prove Proposition
7.4
, we follow the standard construction of
[
Bla85
,
Rae05
,
Lor10
]
. We first need the following
technical lemma.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be a self-similar action of a groupoid on a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources. Let
c\colon(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda)^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
be a normalised categorical
2
-cocycle. Fix
(\mu,g,\nu)
and
(\eta,h,\zeta)
in

\Lambda*\mathcal{G}*\Lambda
, and for each
(\alpha,\beta)
such that
\nu\alpha=\eta\beta\in\MCE(\nu,\eta)
, define

(7.1)

\begin{split}\omega(\alpha,\beta)\coloneqq\overline{c(\nu,\alpha)}&c(\eta,\beta)c(g,\alpha)c(h^{-1},h)\overline{c(h^{-1},\beta)}\,\overline{c(g\triangleleft\alpha,g\triangleright\alpha)}\\
&\times c(h^{-1}\triangleright\beta,h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta)c(\mu,g\triangleright\alpha)\overline{c(\zeta,h^{-1}\triangleright\beta)}\\
&\times\overline{c(h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta,(h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta)^{-1})}c(g\triangleleft\alpha,(h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta)^{-1}).\end{split}

Then for any Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family
T
, we have

T_{\mu}T_{g}T^{*}_{\nu}T_{\eta}T_{h}T^{*}_{\zeta}=\sum_{\nu\alpha=\eta\beta\in\MCE(\nu,\eta)}\omega_{\alpha,\beta}T_{\mu(g\triangleright\alpha)}T_{(g\triangleleft\alpha)(h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta)^{-1}}T^{*}_{\zeta(h^{-1}\triangleright\beta)}.

Proof.

Relation
(TCK3)
implies that each

T^{*}_{\nu}T_{\eta}=T_{\nu}^{*}(T_{\nu}T_{\nu}^{*}T_{\eta}T_{\eta}^{*})T_{\eta}=\sum_{\nu\alpha=\eta\beta\in\MCE(\nu,\eta)}T_{\nu}^{*}T_{\nu\alpha}T_{\eta\beta}^{*}T_{\eta}.

For fixed
(\alpha,\beta)
in the above sum, relations
(TCK1)
and then
(TCK2)
give

T_{\nu}^{*}T_{\nu\alpha}T_{\eta\beta}^{*}T_{\eta}=\overline{c(\nu,\alpha)}c(\eta,\beta)T_{\nu}^{*}T_{\nu}T_{\alpha}T_{\beta}^{*}T_{\eta}^{*}T_{\eta}=\overline{c(\nu,\alpha)}c(\eta,\beta)T_{\alpha}T_{\beta}^{*}.

Fix
\nu\alpha=\eta\beta\in\MCE(\nu,\eta)
. Remark
7.2
gives

T^{*}_{\beta}T_{h}T^{*}_{\zeta}=c(h^{-1},h)T^{*}_{\beta}T^{*}_{h^{-1}}T^{*}_{\zeta}.

We have

T_{g}T_{\alpha}=c(g,\alpha)T_{g\alpha}=c(g,\alpha)T_{(g\triangleright\alpha)(g\triangleleft\alpha)}=c(g,\alpha)\overline{c(g\triangleright\alpha,g\triangleleft\alpha)}T_{g\triangleright\alpha}T_{g\triangleleft\alpha},

and similarly,

T^{*}_{\beta}T^{*}_{h^{-1}}=\overline{c(h^{-1},\beta)}c(h^{-1}\triangleright\beta,h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta)T^{*}_{h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta}T^{*}_{h^{-1}\triangleright\beta}.

We have

T_{\mu}T_{g\triangleright\alpha}=c(\mu,g\triangleright\alpha)T_{\mu(g\triangleright\alpha)}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad T^{*}_{h^{-1}\triangleright\beta}T^{*}_{\zeta}=\overline{c(\zeta,h^{-1}\triangleright\beta)}T^{*}_{\zeta(h^{-1}\triangleright\beta)}.

Finally,

\displaystyle T_{g\triangleright\alpha}T^{*}_{h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta}

\displaystyle=\overline{c(h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta,(h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta)^{-1})}T_{g\triangleright\alpha}T_{(h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta)^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\overline{c(h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta,(h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta)^{-1})}c(g\triangleright\alpha,(h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta)^{-1})T_{(g\triangleright\alpha)(h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta)^{-1}}.

Putting all of these identities together gives

\displaystyle T_{\mu}T_{g}T^{*}_{\nu}T_{\eta}T_{h}T^{*}_{\zeta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\alpha=\eta\beta\in\MCE(\nu,\eta)}\overline{c(\nu,\alpha)}c(\eta,\beta)T_{\mu}T_{g}T_{\alpha}T_{\beta}^{*}T_{h}T^{*}_{\zeta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\alpha=\eta\beta\in\MCE(\nu,\eta)}\omega_{\alpha,\beta}T_{\mu(g\triangleright\alpha)}T_{(g\triangleleft\alpha)(h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta)^{-1}}T^{*}_{\zeta(h^{-1}\triangleright\beta)}.\qed

Corollary 7.6

(cf.
[
LRRW18
, Proposition 4.5]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be a self-similar action of a groupoid on a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources, and let
c\colon(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda)^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
be a normalised categorical
2
-cocycle. If
T
is a Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family, then
C^{*}(T)=\clsp\{T_{\mu}T_{g}T^{*}_{\nu}\colon(\mu,g,\nu)\in\Lambda*\mathcal{G}*\Lambda\}
.

Proof.

The set
X:=\clsp\{T_{\mu}T_{g}T^{*}_{\nu}\colon(\mu,g,\nu)\}
is a closed subspace of
C^{*}(T)
. It is closed under adjoints since
T_{\mu}T_{g}T^{*}_{\nu}=\overline{c(g,g^{-1})}T^{*}_{\nu}T_{g^{-1}}T^{*}_{\mu}
, and Lemma
7.5
shows that it is closed under multiplication, so
X
is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
C^{*}(T)
. Fix
\zeta\in\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda
. Proposition
3.13
gives a unique factorisation
\zeta=\mu g
with
\mu\in\Lambda
and
g\in\mathcal{G}
. So
T_{\zeta}=c(\mu,g)T_{\mu}T_{g}T^{*}_{s(g)}\in X
. So
X
contains the generators of
C^{*}(T)
giving
X=C^{*}(T)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.4
.

Consider the vector
space
V\coloneqq C_{c}(\Lambda*\mathcal{G}*\Lambda)
of finitely supported
complex-valued functions on
\Lambda*\mathcal{G}*\Lambda
, which has basis the
indicator functions
\theta_{\mu,g,\nu}
.

These
\theta_{\mu,g,\nu}
are linearly independent, so there is a conjugate-linear
map
{}^{*}\colon V\to V
such that

(7.2)

\theta^{*}_{\mu,g,\nu}=\overline{c(g,g^{-1})}\theta_{\nu,g,\mu},

and there is a bilinear map
\cdot\colon V\times V\to V
such that, for the
scalars
\omega_{\alpha,\beta}
defined in (
7.1
),

(7.3)

\theta_{\mu,g,\nu}\theta_{\eta,h,\zeta}=\sum_{\nu\alpha=\eta\beta\in\MCE(\nu,\eta)}\omega_{\alpha,\beta}\theta_{\mu(g\triangleright\alpha),(g\triangleleft\alpha)(h^{-1}\triangleleft\beta)^{-1},\zeta(h^{-1}\triangleright\beta)}.

In the Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family
L
of Example
7.3
, the
L_{\mu}L_{g}L^{*}_{\mu}
are linearly independent, so there is a linear injection
\varphi_{L}\colon V\to\mathcal{B}(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda))
satisfying
\varphi_{L}(\theta_{\mu,g,\nu})=L_{\mu}L_{g}L^{*}_{\nu}
. Lemma
7.5
and bilinearity of multiplication in
\mathcal{B}(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda))
shows that
\varphi_{L}
intertwines (
7.3
) with multiplication. Remark
7.2
shows that it carries (
7.2
) to the adjoint in
\mathcal{B}(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda))
.

Since
\mathcal{B}(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda)
is a
∗
-algebra, we deduce that the operations we have defined on
V
satisfy the
∗
-algebra axioms, so
V
is a
∗
-algebra.
The
\theta_{\mu,g,\nu}
are linearly independent, so for any
Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family
T
there is a linear map

\varphi_{T}\colon V\to\clsp\{T_{\mu}T_{g}T^{*}_{\nu}\colon(\mu,g,\nu)\in\Lambda*\mathcal{G}*\Lambda\}
such that
\varphi_{T}(\theta_{\mu,g,\nu})=T_{\mu}T_{g}T^{*}_{\nu}
.
Lemma
7.5
shows that
\varphi_{T}
is a homomorphism. The

T_{\zeta}
are partial isometries, so for
a=\sum_{(\mu,g,\nu)}a_{\mu,g,\nu}\theta_{\mu,g,\nu}\in V
, we have

\|\varphi_{T}(a)\|\leq\sum_{(\mu,g,\nu)}|a_{\mu,g,\nu}|
. The map
\rho:V\to[0,\infty)
, given by

\rho(a)\coloneqq\sup\{\|\varphi_{T}(a)\|\colon T\text{ is a Toeplitz--Cuntz--Krieger $(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)$-family}\}

is a pre-
C^{*}
-seminorm. Quotienting by
N\coloneqq\ker\rho
and
completing gives a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
.

The map
t\colon\mu g\mapsto\overline{c(\mu,g)}\theta_{\mu,g,s(g)}+N
is a Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family
in
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
because
N
contains the obstructions to relations
(TCK1)
–
(TCK3)
. This
t
is universal:
for any family
T
and any
a\in V
, we have
\|\varphi_{T}(a)\|\leq\|\varphi_{t}(a)\|
, so
\varphi_{T}
factors through a norm-decreasing
homomorphism from
\varphi_{t}(V)
to
\varphi_{T}(V)
, which extends to a homomorphism
\pi^{T}\colon\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\Lambda,\mathcal{G};c)\to C^{*}(T)
of

C^{*}
-algebras by continuity.

By definition of
I
, the map
s

is a Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family. Given any Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family
S
, the kernel of the homomorphism

\pi^{S}\colon\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\Lambda,\mathcal{G};c)\to C^{*}(S)
contains
I
, so
\pi^{S}
descends to a homomorphism
C^{*}(\Lambda,\mathcal{G};c)\to C^{*}(S)
.
∎

Since
\Lambda
and
\mathcal{G}
are subcategories of
\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda
, if
c\colon(\Lambda*\mathcal{G})^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
is a
2
-cocycle then

c|_{\Lambda^{2}}
and
c|_{\mathcal{G}^{2}}
are
2
-cocycles on
\Lambda
and
\mathcal{G}
, which we continue to denote by
c
.

Our next steps are to show that if
\mathcal{G}
is amenable, then
\iota^{t}_{\mathcal{G}}\colon C^{*}(\mathcal{G},c)\to\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
from Remark
7.2
is always injective, and follow Yusnitha’s analysis

[
Yus23
]
to see that her joint-faithfulness condition implies that
\iota^{s}_{\mathcal{G}}\colon C^{*}(\mathcal{G},c)\to C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
is faithful. We also show that

\iota^{t}_{\Lambda}\colon\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\Lambda,c)\to\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
and

\iota^{s}_{\Lambda}\colon C^{*}(\Lambda,c)\to C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
are always
injective.

If
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
is a self-similar action of a groupoid on a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources, then the degree map
d_{\Lambda}:\Lambda\to\mathbb{N}^{k}
determines a function
d_{\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda}:\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda\to\mathbb{N}^{k}
by
d_{\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda}(\mu,g)=d_{\Lambda}(\mu)
for all
(\mu,g)\in\Lambda*\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda
. We will just write
d
for both
d_{\Lambda}
and
d_{\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda}
unless the subscript is needed for clarity. Since
d(g\triangleright\mu)=d(\mu)
for all
(g,\mu)\in\mathcal{G}*\Lambda
, for each
((\mu,g),(\nu,h))\in(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda)^{2}
,

d((\mu,g)(\nu,h))=d\big(\mu(g\triangleright\nu),(g\triangleleft\nu)h\big)=d(\mu(g\triangleright\nu))=d(\mu\nu)=d(\mu,g)+d(\nu,h).

So
d:\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda\to\mathbb{N}^{k}
is a functor.

For each
z\in\mathbb{T}^{k}
, the
function
\gamma_{z}(t)\colon\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda\to\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
defined
by
\gamma_{z}(t)(\zeta)=z^{d(\zeta)}t_{\zeta}
is a Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger

(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family. By the universal property,
\gamma_{z}

extends to an endomorphism of
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
. Since
\gamma_{z}\circ\gamma_{w}(s_{\zeta})=\gamma_{zw}(s_{\zeta})
for all
\zeta\in\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda
, this

\gamma
is an action by automorphisms. An
\varepsilon/3
-argument shows that it is
strongly continuous. We call
\gamma\colon\mathbb{T}^{k}\to\Aut(\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c))

the
gauge action
, and write
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)^{\gamma}
for the fixed-point
algebra
\{a\in\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)\mid\gamma_{z}(a)=a\text{ for all }z\in\mathbb{T}\}
.

The same argument yields a strongly continuous action, also denoted
\gamma
and called the gauge action, of
\mathbb{T}
on
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
such that
\gamma_{z}(s_{\zeta})=z^{d(\zeta)}s_{\zeta}
for all
\zeta
, and we likewise write
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)^{\gamma}
for the resulting fixed-point algebra.

Proposition 7.7

(cf.
[
Yus23
, Proposition 3.6]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be a self-similar action of a groupoid on a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources, and let
c\colon(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda)^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
be a
normalised categorical
2
-cocycle. The generators
t_{\zeta}
of
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
and
s_{\zeta}
of
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)

are all nonzero. The homomorphisms
\iota^{t}_{\Lambda}:\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\Lambda,c)\to\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
and

\iota^{s}_{\Lambda}:C^{*}(\Lambda,c)\to C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
are injective.
If
\mathcal{G}
is amenable, then
\iota^{t}_{\mathcal{G}}\colon C^{*}(\mathcal{G},c)\to\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
is injective. If, in addition, for
every
v\in\Lambda^{0}
and every
n\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, there exists
\lambda\in v\Lambda^{n}
such that
g\mapsto(g\triangleleft\lambda,g\triangleright\lambda)
is
injective on
\mathcal{G}^{v}_{v}
, then
\iota^{s}_{\mathcal{G}}\colon C^{*}(\mathcal{G},c)\to C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
is injective.

Proof.

Let
L\colon\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda\to\mathcal{B}(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda))
be the Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family of Example
7.3
. Since the
L_{v}
are nonzero, the universal property of
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
implies that the
t_{v}
are nonzero. As each
\|t_{\zeta}\|^{2}=\|t_{\zeta}^{*}t_{\zeta}\|=\|t_{s(\zeta)}\|
, the
t_{\zeta}
are all nonzero. For each
v\in\Lambda^{0}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, we have
(L_{v}-\sum_{\mu\in v\Lambda^{n}}L_{\mu}L^{*}_{\mu})e_{v}=e_{v}\not=0
, so
[
SWW14
, Theorem 3.15]
implies that
\Pi^{L}\circ\iota^{t}_{\Lambda}
is injective, and hence
\iota^{t}_{\Lambda}
itself is injective.

To see that the
s_{\zeta}
are all nonzero, we follow the argument of
[
Yus23
]
. For each
v\in\Lambda^{0}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, the projection
\Delta_{n,v}\coloneqq L_{v}-\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{n}}L_{\lambda}L^{*}_{\lambda}
vanishes on
\clsp\{e_{\lambda g}\colon d(\lambda)\geq n\}
. A direct calculation using
that
d(g\triangleright\lambda)=d(\lambda)
for all
\lambda
, shows that
L_{\lambda}L_{g}L^{*}_{\mu}\Delta_{n,v}L_{\nu}L_{h}L_{\eta}^{*}e_{\zeta g}=0
whenever
d(\zeta)>d(\eta)
and
d(\zeta)-d(\eta)+d(\nu)\geq n
. In particular, for
a\in\lsp\{L_{\lambda}L_{g}L^{*}_{\mu}\Delta_{n,v}L_{\nu}L_{h}L_{\eta}^{*}\colon\lambda,\mu,\nu,\eta\in\Lambda,g,h\in\mathcal{G},n\in\mathbb{N}^{k},v\in\Lambda^{0}\}
,
regarding
\mathbb{N}^{k}
as a directed set,
\lim_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{k}}\big\|a|_{\clsp\{e_{\zeta g}\colon\zeta\in\Lambda^{n},g\in\mathcal{G}\}}\big\|=0
. An approximation argument gives

(7.4)

\lim_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{k}}\big\|\pi^{L}(a)|_{\clsp\{e_{\zeta g}\colon\zeta\in\Lambda^{n},g\in\mathcal{G}\}}\big\|=0\quad\text{ for all $a\in I$.}

Fix
v\in\Lambda^{0}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, and
\zeta\in v\Lambda^{n}
. Then
\|L_{v}e_{\zeta}\|=\|e_{\zeta}\|=1
, and so

\lim_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{k}}\big\|\pi^{L}(t_{v})|_{\clsp\{e_{\zeta g}\colon\zeta\in\Lambda^{n},g\in\mathcal{G}\}}\big\|=1.

Hence,
t_{v}\not\in I
, so
s_{v}=t_{v}+I\neq 0
. Now by
(TCK2)
, each
\|s_{\zeta}\|^{2}=\|s_{\zeta}s^{*}_{\zeta}\|=\|s_{s(\zeta)}\|>0
. The homomorphism
\iota^{s}_{\Lambda}
intertwines the gauge actions of
\mathbb{T}^{k}
on
C^{*}(\Lambda,c)
and
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
, so the gauge-invariant uniqueness theorem
[
KPS15
, Corollary 7.7]
implies that
\iota^{s}_{\Lambda}
is injective.

The subspace
\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G})\subseteq\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda)
is invariant for
\pi^{L}_{\mathcal{G}}\colon C^{*}(\mathcal{G},c)\to\mathcal{B}(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda))
, and the reduction of
\pi^{L}
to
\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G})
is the
left regular representation
\lambda
of
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},c)
. Since
\mathcal{G}
is amenable,
\lambda
is faithful, so
\pi^{L}_{\mathcal{G}}=\pi^{L}\circ\iota^{t}_{\mathcal{G}}
is injective, and hence
\iota^{t}_{\mathcal{G}}
is injective.

Finally, fix
v\in\Lambda^{0}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, and

\lambda\in\Lambda
such that
g\mapsto(g\triangleright\lambda,g\triangleleft\lambda)
is
injective on
\mathcal{G}^{r(\lambda)}_{r(\lambda)}
. Again following Yusnitha

[
Yus23
, Proposition 3.6]
, the space
\mathcal{H}_{n,\lambda}\coloneqq\clsp\{e_{g\lambda\colon g\in\mathcal{G}^{v}_{v}}\}
is invariant for
\{L_{g}\colon g\in\mathcal{G}^{v}_{v}\}
, and
e_{g\lambda}\mapsto\overline{c(g,\lambda)}e_{g}
induces an isomorphism
U\colon\mathcal{H}_{n,\lambda}\to\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{v}_{v})
that intertwines the
reduction of
\pi^{L}|_{C^{*}(\mathcal{G}^{v}_{v},c)}
with the regular representation. Since
\mathcal{G}
is amenable, so is
\mathcal{G}^{v}_{v}
and so the reduction of
\pi^{L}|_{C^{*}(\mathcal{G}^{v}_{v},c)}
to
\mathcal{H}_{n,\lambda}
is faithful. So, for
a\in C^{*}(\mathcal{G}^{v}_{v},c)
, we have
\big\|\pi^{L}(a)|_{\clsp\{e_{\zeta g}\colon\zeta\in\Lambda^{n},g\in\mathcal{G}\}}\big\|=\|a\|
for all
n
. So by (
7.4
),
a\not\in I
, so
\iota^{s}_{\mathcal{G}}
is injective on each
C^{*}(\mathcal{G}^{v}_{v},c)
.

Fix a subset
V\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{0}
that intersects each

\mathcal{G}
-orbit exactly once. Then
P_{V}=\sum_{v\in V}\delta_{v}\in\mathcal{M}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},c)
is a full projection, and
P_{V}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},c)P_{V}\cong\bigoplus_{v\in V}C^{*}(\mathcal{G}^{v}_{v},c)
. Since
\iota_{s}^{\mathcal{G}}
is injective
on this full corner, it is injective on all of
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},c)
.
∎

Remark 7.8
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be a self-similar action of an amenable groupoid on a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources, and let
c:(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda)^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
be a normalised categorical
2
-cocycle. Proposition
7.7
shows that
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
is generated copies of
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\Lambda;c)
and
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},c)
. It would be interesting to determine when
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
is a
C^{*}
-blend of these two subalgebras in the sense of
[
Exe13
]
; or when
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
is a blend of
C^{*}(\Lambda,c)
and
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},c)
(the corresponding result for Zappa–Szép products of Fell bundles over groupoids appears in
[
DL23
, Theorem 5.4]
). For example, it seems likely that a contracting condition like that of
[
LRRW18
, Section 9]
or
[
Nek05
, Section 2.11]
implies that each spanning element
s_{\mu}s_{g}s^{*}_{\nu}
of
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
belongs to
\clsp\{s_{\alpha}s^{*}_{\beta}s_{g}\mid\alpha,\beta\in\Lambda,g\in\mathcal{G}\}
. But we do not pursue this question here.

We now prove a gauge-invariant uniqueness theorem for
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
. This by-now ubiquitous tool in the study of
C^{*}
-algebras of graphs and related objects goes back to
[
aHR97
]
. Our argument in the context of twisted
C^{*}
-algebras of self-similar actions on
k
-graphs generalises those of
[
LRRW18
,
ABRW19
,
Yus23
]
for untwisted actions on graphs and
k
-graphs; our analysis of the core is heavily based on Yusnitha’s
[
Yus23
]
.

Proposition 7.9

(cf.
[
Yus23
, Lemma 4.10]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be a self-similar groupoid action on a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources. Let
c\colon(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda)^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
be a normalised categorical
2
-cocycle. Then

C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)^{\gamma}=\clsp\big\{s_{\mu}s_{g}s^{*}_{\nu}\colon\mu,\nu\in\Lambda,d(\mu)=d(\nu),g\in\mathcal{G}^{s(\mu)}_{s(\nu)}\big\}.

Let
S
be a Cuntz–Krieger
(\Lambda,\mathcal{G};c)
-family, and suppose that
\pi^{S}\colon C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)\to C^{*}(S)
is injective on
\iota^{s}_{\mathcal{G}}(C^{*}(\mathcal{G},c))
. Then
\pi^{S}
is injective on
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)^{\gamma}
.

Proof.

Let
\Phi\colon C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)\to C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)^{\gamma}
be the faithful conditional
expectation satisfying
\Phi(a)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{k}}\gamma_{z}(a)\,dz

[
Rae05
, Proposition 3.2]
. Then
\Phi(s_{\mu}s_{g}s^{*}_{\nu})=\delta_{d(\mu),d(\nu)}s_{\mu}s_{g}s^{*}_{\nu}
, and so

\displaystyle C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)^{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\Phi(C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c))=\Phi\big(\clsp\big\{s_{\mu}s_{g}s^{*}_{\nu}\colon\mu,\nu\in\Lambda,g\in\mathcal{G}^{s(\mu)}_{s(\nu)}\big\}\big)

\displaystyle=\clsp\big\{s_{\mu}s_{g}s^{*}_{\nu}\colon\mu,\nu\in\Lambda,d(\mu)=d(\nu),g\in\mathcal{G}^{s(\mu)}_{s(\nu)}\big\}.

For fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
,

F_{n}\coloneqq\clsp\big\{s_{\mu}s_{g}s^{*}_{\nu}\colon\mu,\nu\in\Lambda^{n},g\in\mathcal{G}^{s(\mu)}_{s(\nu)}\big\}

is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)^{\gamma}
. If
m,n\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
,
\mu,\nu\in\Lambda^{m}
, and
g\in\mathcal{G}_{s(\nu)}^{s(\mu)}
, then

\displaystyle s_{\mu}s_{g}s^{*}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=s_{\mu}s_{g}\sum_{\tau\in s(g)\Lambda^{n}}s_{\tau}s^{*}_{\tau}s^{*}_{\nu}=\sum_{\tau\in s(g)\Lambda^{n}}c(g,\tau)\overline{c(g\triangleright\tau,g\triangleleft\tau)}s_{\mu}s_{g\triangleright\tau}s_{g\triangleleft\tau}s^{*}_{\tau}s^{*}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in s(g)\Lambda^{n}}c(\mu,g\triangleright\tau)\overline{c(\nu,\tau)}c(g,\tau)\overline{c(g\triangleright\tau,g\triangleleft\tau)}s_{\mu g\triangleright\tau}s_{g\triangleleft\tau}s^{*}_{\nu\tau}\in F_{m+n}.

So
F_{m}\subseteq F_{m+n}
and
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)^{\gamma}=\overline{\bigcup_{n}F_{n}}
. So to see that
\pi^{S}
is
injective on
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)^{\gamma}
, it suffices to show that it is
injective, and hence isometric, on each
F_{n}
.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
. Consider the equivalence relation
\sim
on
\Lambda^{n}
such that
\lambda\sim\mu
if and only if
\mathcal{G}^{s(\lambda)}_{s(\mu)}\not=\varnothing
. Let
K\subseteq\Lambda^{n}
be a set of representatives of
\Lambda^{n}/{\sim}
. For
\lambda\in\Lambda^{n}
, there exists
\mu\in K
with
\mu\sim\lambda
, say
g\in\mathcal{G}^{s(\lambda)}_{s(\mu)}
. So
s_{\lambda}s^{*}_{\lambda}=s_{\lambda}u_{g}s^{*}_{\mu}(s_{\mu}s^{*}_{\mu})s_{\mu}u_{g}^{*}s_{\lambda}
. Since
\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda^{n}}s_{\lambda}s^{*}_{\lambda}
is an approximate identity for
F_{n}
it
follows that
P_{K}\coloneqq\sum_{\lambda\in K}s_{\lambda}s^{*}_{\lambda}
is a full
projection in
\mathcal{M}F_{n}
. So it suffices to show that
\pi^{S}
is injective on

P_{K}F_{n}P_{K}
.

For distinct
\lambda,\mu\in K
we have
s_{\lambda}s^{*}_{\lambda}F_{k}s_{\mu}s^{*}_{\mu}=\{0\}
by definition of
\sim
, and so
P_{k}F_{k}P_{k}\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\in K}s_{\lambda}s^{*}_{\lambda}F_{k}s_{\lambda}s^{*}_{\lambda}
.
So it suffices to show that
\pi^{S}
is injective on each
s_{\lambda}s^{*}_{\lambda}F_{k}s_{\lambda}s^{*}_{\lambda}
.

Fix
\lambda\in K
, and let
v\coloneqq s(\lambda)
. Since the
s_{\mu}s^{*}_{\mu}
, for
\mu\in\Lambda^{n}
, are mutually orthogonal,
s_{\lambda}s^{*}_{\lambda}F_{k}s_{\lambda}s^{*}_{\lambda}=\clsp\{s_{\lambda}s_{g}s^{*}_{\lambda}\colon g\in\mathcal{G}^{v}_{v}\}
. Conjugation by
s_{\lambda}
is an isomorphism of this
subalgebra onto
\clsp\{s_{g}\colon g\in\mathcal{G}^{v}_{v}\}
. Similarly,
\clsp\{S_{\lambda}S_{g}S^{*}_{\lambda}\colon g\in\mathcal{G}^{v}_{v}\}\cong\clsp\{S_{g}\colon g\in\mathcal{G}^{v}_{v}\}
via conjugation by
S_{\lambda}
. Since
\pi^{S}(s_{\lambda})=S_{\lambda}
, and
\pi^{S}

is injective on
\clsp\{s_{g}\colon g\in\mathcal{G}^{v}_{v}\}
, the result follows.
∎

We obtain a version of an Huef and Raeburn’s gauge-invariant uniqueness theorem
[
aHR97
]
.

Corollary 7.10

(The Gauge-Invariant Uniqueness Theorem)
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be a self-similar action of a groupoid on a row-finite
k
graph with no sources, and let
c\colon(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda)^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
be a normalised categorical
2
-cocycle. Let
S
be a Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
family in a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
. If there is a strongly-continuous action
\beta\colon\mathbb{T}^{k}\to\Aut(A)
such that
\beta_{z}(S_{\zeta})=z^{d(\zeta)}s_{\zeta}
for all
\zeta\in\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda
, and if

\pi^{S}\colon C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)\to C^{*}(S)
is injective on
\iota^{s}_{\mathcal{G}}(C^{*}(\mathcal{G},c))
, then
\pi^{S}
is injective.

Proof.

The assumptions combined with Proposition
7.9
show that
\pi^{S}
is injective on
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)^{\gamma}
. Define
\Gamma\colon A\to A
by
\Gamma(a)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{k}}\beta_{z}(a)\,dz
.
Since
\beta_{z}\circ\pi^{S}=\pi^{S}\circ\gamma_{z}

for all
z
, we have
\pi^{S}\circ\Phi=\Gamma\circ\pi^{S}
, and then
[
SWW14
, Lemma 3.14]
shows that
\pi^{S}
is injective.
∎

We now show that the isomorphism class of the twisted
C^{*}
-algebra of a self-similar action of a groupoid on a
k
-graph depends only on the cohomology class of the twisting
2
-cocycle. The argument is standard; see, for example,
[
KPS15
, Proposition 5.6]
.

Proposition 7.11
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be a self-similar action on a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources, and let
c_{1},c_{2}\colon(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda)^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
be normalised categorical
2
-cocycles. Suppose that
b\colon\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda\to\mathbb{T}
is a categorical
1
-cochain such that
d_{\bowtie}^{1}(b)c_{1}=c_{2}
. For
i=1,2
let
t^{i}
be the universal
Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger family in
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c_{i})
. Then there
is an isomorphism
\theta_{b}\colon\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c_{2})\to\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda,c_{1})
such that
\theta_{b}(t^{2}_{\zeta})=b(\zeta)t^{1}_{\zeta}
for all

\zeta\in\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda
. This
isomorphism descends to an isomorphism

\widetilde{\theta}_{b}\colon C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c_{2})\to C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda,c_{1})
.

Proof.

Define
bt\colon\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda\to\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c_{1})
by

(bt)_{\zeta}\coloneqq b(\zeta)t^{1}_{\zeta}.

For
(\zeta,\eta)\in(\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda)^{2}
,

(bt)_{\zeta}(bt)_{\eta}=b(\zeta)t^{1}_{\zeta}b(\eta)t^{1}_{\eta}=b(\zeta\eta)d^{1}(b)(\zeta,\eta)c_{1}(\zeta,\eta)t^{1}_{\zeta\eta}=c_{2}(\zeta,\eta)(bt)_{\zeta\eta}.

So
bt
satisfies
(TCK1)
. It also satisfies

(TCK2)
and
(TCK3)
, and satisfies
(CK)
if and only if
t
does,
because the factors of
b(\zeta)
and
\overline{b(\zeta)}
in these relations cancel.

The universal property of
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c_{2})
gives a homomorphism

\theta_{b}\colon\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c_{2})\to\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda,c_{1})
such
that
\theta_{b}(t^{2}_{\zeta})=b(\zeta)t^{1}_{\zeta}
for all
\zeta\in\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda
, which descends to a homomorphism
\widetilde{\theta}_{b}\colon C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c_{2})\to C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda,c_{1})
. Since
d^{1}(\overline{b})c_{2}=c_{1}
, there is a corresponding homomorphism
\theta_{\overline{b}}\colon\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c_{1})\to\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda,c_{2})
such that

\theta_{\overline{b}}(t^{2}_{\zeta})=\overline{b(\zeta)}t^{1}_{\zeta}
, which also
descends to Cuntz–Krieger algebras. Since
\theta_{b}\circ\theta_{\overline{b}}
and
\theta_{\overline{b}}\circ\theta_{b}
fix the
generators
t^{i}_{\zeta}
, they are the identity homomorphisms, and this descends to Cuntz–Krieger algebras as well.
∎

7.2.
Twists by total 2-cocycles

We describe the twisted
C^{*}
-algebra of a self-similar action on
k
-graph with respect to a total
2
-cocycle, and show that we obtain the same class of
C^{*}
-algebras as for categorical cohomology.

Definition 7.12
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be a self-similar action of a groupoid on a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources. A function
\varphi\colon\mathcal{G}^{2}\sqcup(\mathcal{G}*\Lambda)\sqcup\Lambda^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
is a
normalised total
\mathbb{T}
-valued
2
-cocycle
on
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
, if
\varphi_{2,0}\coloneqq\varphi|_{\mathcal{G}^{2}}
,

\varphi_{1,1}\coloneqq\varphi|_{\mathcal{G}*\Lambda}
and
\varphi_{0,2}\coloneqq\varphi|_{\Lambda^{2}}
satisfy

(i)

\varphi_{2,0}\colon\mathcal{G}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
is a normalised
\mathbb{T}
-valued
2
-cocycle in the
sense of
[
Ren80
]
;

(ii)

\varphi_{0,2}\colon\Lambda^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
is a normalised
\mathbb{T}
-valued categorical

2
-cocycle in the sense of
[
KPS15
]
; and

(iii)

\varphi_{1,1}(h,\lambda)=1
whenever
h\in\mathcal{G}^{0}
, or
\lambda\in\Lambda^{0}
, and for
(g,h,\lambda,\mu)\in\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{G}*\Lambda*\Lambda
,

\displaystyle\varphi_{1,1}(h\triangleleft\lambda,\mu)\overline{\varphi_{1,1}(h,\lambda\mu)}\varphi_{1,1}(h,\lambda)\varphi_{0,2}(\lambda,\mu)\overline{\varphi_{0,2}(h\triangleright(\lambda,\mu))}

\displaystyle=1\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\varphi_{2,0}((g,h)\triangleleft\lambda)\overline{\varphi_{2,0}(g,h)}\,\overline{\varphi_{1,1}(h,\lambda)}\varphi_{1,1}(gh,\lambda)\overline{\varphi_{1,1}(g,h\triangleright\lambda)}

\displaystyle=1.

Remark 7.13
.

In defining a normalised total
\mathbb{T}
-valued 2-cocycle we have just written out explicitly what it means for
\varphi
to be a cocycle in
C^{2}_{\Tot}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;\mathbb{T})
. This can be verified by computing what it means for a cochain to be in the kernel
d^{2}_{\Tot}\colon C^{2}_{\Tot}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;\mathbb{T})\to C^{3}_{\Tot}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;\mathbb{T})
which satisfies
d^{2}_{\Tot}\circ\varphi=\varphi\circ d_{2}^{\Tot}
, where
d_{2}^{\Tot}
is from Section
4.4
.

Definition 7.14
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be a self-similar action of a groupoid on a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources. Fix a normalised cocycle
\varphi\in C^{2}_{\Tot}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;\mathbb{T})
and let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. A pair of functions
\mathfrak{t}\colon\Lambda\to A
and
\mathfrak{w}\colon\mathcal{G}\to A
is a
Toeplitz

\varphi
-pair
if:

(T1)

\mathfrak{t}
is a Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\Lambda,\varphi_{0,2})
-family in the sense of
[
SWW14
]
,

(T2)

\mathfrak{w}
is a unitary representation of
(\mathcal{G},\varphi_{2,0})
,
and

(T3)

\mathfrak{w}_{g}\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}=\varphi_{1,1}(g,\lambda)\mathfrak{t}_{g\triangleright\lambda}\mathfrak{w}_{g\triangleleft\lambda}
for all

(g,\lambda)\in\mathcal{G}*\Lambda
.

We call
(\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{t})
a
Cuntz–Krieger
\varphi
-pair

if
\mathfrak{t}
is a Cuntz–Krieger
(\Lambda,\varphi_{0,2})
-family.

With
\Psi_{\bullet}\colon C_{\bullet}^{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)\to C_{\bullet}^{\Tot}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
as in Subsection
5.1.3
,
define
\Psi^{\bullet}\colon C^{\bullet}_{\Tot}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;\mathbb{T})\to C^{\bullet}_{\bowtie}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;\mathbb{T})
by

\Psi^{k}=\varphi\circ\Psi_{k}
. Then
\Psi^{\bullet}
induces an isomorphism on cohomology. On
2
-cochains,

\Psi^{2}(\varphi)(\lambda g,\mu h)=\varphi_{2,0}(g\triangleleft\mu,h)\varphi_{1,1}(g,\mu)\varphi_{0,2}(\lambda,g\triangleright\mu)

for all
(\lambda,g,\mu,h)\in\Lambda*\mathcal{G}*\Lambda*\mathcal{G}
.

We show that
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;\Psi^{2}(\varphi))
is universal for Toeplitz
\varphi
-pairs,
and
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;\Psi^{2}(\varphi))
is universal for Cuntz–Krieger
\varphi
-pairs.

Theorem 7.15
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be a self-similar action of a groupoid on a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources. Fix a normalised cocycle
\varphi\in C^{2}_{\Tot}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;\mathbb{T})
and let
c\coloneqq\Psi^{2}(\varphi)\in C_{\bowtie}^{2}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;\mathbb{T})
.

(i)

Let
t\colon\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda\to\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
be the universal Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family. There is a Toeplitz
\varphi
-pair
\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{w}
in
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)

given by
\mathfrak{t}=t|_{\Lambda}
and
\mathfrak{w}=t|_{\mathcal{G}}
. Moreover
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
is generated by the ranges of
\mathfrak{t}
and
\mathfrak{w}
, and is universal in the sense that given any Toeplitz
\varphi
-pair

\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
in a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, there is a homomorphism
\rho\colon\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)\to A
such that
\rho\circ\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
and
\rho\circ\mathfrak{w}=\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

Let
s\colon\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda\to C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
be the universal Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family. Then there is a Cuntz–Krieger
\varphi
-pair
\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{u}
in
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
given by
\mathfrak{s}=s|_{\Lambda}
and
\mathfrak{u}=s|_{\mathcal{G}}
. Moreover
C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
is generated by the ranges of
\mathfrak{s}
and
\mathfrak{u}
, and is universal in the sense that given any Cuntz–Krieger

\varphi
-pair
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}
in a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, there is a homomorphism
\rho\colon C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)\to A
such that
\rho\circ\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
and
\rho\circ\mathfrak{u}=\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}
.

The theorem follows from the following correspondence between
\varphi
pairs and
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-families.

Lemma 7.16
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be a self-similar action of a groupoid on a row-finite
k
-graph with no sources. Fix a normalised cocycle
\varphi\in C^{2}_{\Tot}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;\mathbb{T})
and let
c\coloneqq\Psi^{2}(\varphi)\in C_{\bowtie}^{2}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;\mathbb{T})
. If
\mathfrak{t}
,
\mathfrak{w}
is a Toeplitz
\varphi
-pair in a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, then

t_{\lambda g}\coloneqq\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}\mathfrak{w}_{g}

defines a Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family in
A
. If
t
is a Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family in
A
, then
\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}\coloneqq t_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in\Lambda
and

\mathfrak{w}_{g}\coloneqq t_{g}
for
g\in\mathcal{G}
defines a Toeplitz
\varphi
-pair. Moreover,
\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{w}
is a Cuntz–Krieger
\varphi
-pair if and only if
t
is a Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family.

Proof.

For
g\in\mathcal{G}
and
\lambda\in s(g)\Lambda
,

\displaystyle c(g,\lambda)=c(r(g)g,\lambda s(\lambda))=\varphi_{2,0}(g\triangleleft\lambda,s(\lambda))\varphi_{1,1}(g,\lambda)\varphi_{0,2}(r(g),g\triangleright\lambda)=\varphi_{1,1}(g,\lambda).

Also, for
h\in\mathcal{G}
and
\mu\in\Lambda r(h)
, since
v\triangleleft v=v\triangleright v=v
for
v\in\Lambda^{0}=\mathcal{G}^{0}
, we have

c(\mu,h)=c(\mu s(\mu),r(h)h)=\varphi_{2,0}(r(h),h)\varphi_{1,1}(s(\mu),r(h))\varphi_{0,2}(\mu,s(\mu))=1.

Hence, if
g\in\mathcal{G}
and
\lambda\in s(g)\Lambda
, we have

(7.5)

c(g,\lambda)\overline{c(g\triangleright\lambda,g\triangleleft\lambda)}=c(g,\lambda)1=\varphi_{1,1}(g,\lambda).

Suppose that
\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{w}
is a Toeplitz
\varphi
-pair. If
\lambda g,\mu h
are composable in
\mathcal{G}\bowtie\Lambda
, then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}\mathfrak{w}_{g}\mathfrak{t}_{\mu}\mathfrak{w}_{h}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{1,1}(g,\mu)\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}\mathfrak{t}_{g\triangleright\mu}\mathfrak{w}_{g\triangleleft\mu}\mathfrak{w}_{h}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{2,0}(\lambda,g\triangleright\mu)\varphi_{1,1}(g,\mu)\varphi_{0,2}(g\triangleleft\mu,h)\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda(g\triangleright\mu)}\mathfrak{w}_{(g\triangleleft\mu)h}

\displaystyle=c(\lambda g,\mu h)\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda(g\triangleright\mu)}\mathfrak{w}_{(g\triangleleft\mu)h},

so
t\colon\lambda g\mapsto\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}\mathfrak{w}_{g}
satisfies
(TCK1)
. For
(TCK2)
, we calculate

t_{\lambda g}^{*}t_{\lambda g}=\mathfrak{w}_{g}^{*}\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}^{*}\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}\mathfrak{w}_{g}=\mathfrak{w}_{g}^{*}\mathfrak{t}_{s(\lambda)}\mathfrak{w}_{g}=\mathfrak{w}_{g}^{*}\mathfrak{w}_{g}=\mathfrak{w}_{s(g)}=s_{s(\lambda g)}.

Relation
(TCK3)
follows from
(T1)
by definition of a Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\Lambda,\varphi_{0,2})
-family.

Now suppose that
t
is a Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family, and define
\mathfrak{t}=t|_{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathfrak{w}=t|_{\Lambda}
. We have
\mathfrak{t}_{v}=\mathfrak{w}_{v}
for
v\in\Lambda^{0}
because
\mathcal{G}^{0}=\Lambda^{0}
.

Remark
7.2
implies that
\mathfrak{t}
and
\mathfrak{w}
satisfy
(T1)
and
(T2)
. For
(T3)
, we calculate

\mathfrak{w}_{g}\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}=t_{g}t_{\lambda}\overset{\eqref{eq:psivarphi on g,lambda}}{=}c(g,\lambda)\overline{c(g\triangleright\lambda,g\triangleleft\lambda)}t_{g\triangleright\lambda}t_{g\triangleleft\lambda}=\varphi_{1,1}(g,\lambda)\mathfrak{t}_{g\triangleright\lambda}\mathfrak{w}_{g\triangleleft\lambda}.

So
\mathfrak{t}
,
\mathfrak{w}
is a Toeplitz
\varphi
-pair.
For the final assertion, observe that

\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{n}}t_{\lambda}t^{*}_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{n}}\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}\mathfrak{w}_{s(\lambda)}\mathfrak{w}_{s(\lambda)}^{*}\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda\in v\Lambda^{n}}\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{\lambda}.\qed

Proof of Theorem
7.15
.

(i)
The second statement of Lemma
7.16
shows that
\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{w}
is a Toeplitz
\varphi
-pair. It generates
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
because each
t_{\lambda g}=c(\lambda,g)t_{\lambda}t_{g}=c(\lambda,g)\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}\mathfrak{w}_{g}
; and given a Toeplitz
\varphi
-pair
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
, the first statement of Lemma
7.16
shows that

t^{\prime}_{\lambda g}\coloneqq\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}_{\lambda}\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}_{g}
defines a Toeplitz–Cuntz–Krieger
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
-family. So the universal property of
\mathcal{T}C^{*}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda;c)
gives a homomorphism
\rho
such that

\rho(t_{\lambda g})=t^{\prime}_{\lambda g}
. In particular,
\rho(\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda})=\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}_{\lambda}
, and
\rho(\mathfrak{w}_{g})=\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}_{g}
.

(ii)
Apply
(i)
together with the final statement of Lemma
7.16
.
∎
